                     NOTICE OF PUBLIC HEARING BEFORE THE PLANNING COMMISSION

The Provo City Planning Commission will hold a public hearing on June 24, 2026 at 6:00 PM. Located at:
Council Chambers 445 West Center Street. The items listed below will be discussed, and anyone interested
is invited to participate and provide comment. Hearings can be viewed live and on-demand at: YouTube
youtube.com/provocitygovernment and on Facebook facebook.com/provochannel17.

                     NOTICE OF PUBLIC MEETING BEFORE PROVO MUNICIPAL COUNCIL

On Tuesday, July 14, 2026, at 5:30 PM. the Provo Municipal Council will consider the items noted below
with a star (*). Items noted on the agendas with a star require legislative action by the Municipal Council.
Council agendas can be viewed at the Provo City Council web site on the Thursday prior to the Council
meeting at http://agendas.provo.gov. For more information, call (801) 852-6120.

Unmarked items are administrative and require the approval only of the Planning Commission. Decisions
on the unmarked items may be appealed to the Board of Adjustment by making application by 6:00 PM.
within 14 days of the Planning Commission decision.

Study Session
1. Continued Review of Title 14 rewrite, Chapters 13-23 – Bill Peperone

                            APPLICANT:       R. Jay Henrie
                  PROPERTY OWNER:            GENEVA 10 LLC,
                            PARCEL ID:       19:046:0018, 19:046:0046
                             ACREAGE:        10.81
               CURRENT LEGAL USE:            Single-family Residential

NEIGHBORHOOD FEEDBACK
The West District meeting was held on May 6, 2026, and this item was presented.
Residents had concerns about twin homes in this area, and it was confirmed that there would
only be 2,500-3,000 square foot single-family homes. These homes would be without
basements due to the high-water table.

PROJECT SUMMARY
The applicant has submitted this zone map amendment request along with a preliminary plat
application showing how the property would be subdivided into single-family, residential lots if
the rezone is approved. The property is currently zoned for single-family, but the R1.20 zone
requires a minimum 20,000 square foot lot size. The applicant would like to develop smaller
single-family lots on this large plot of land, and this is why he is looking to have the property
rezoned.
The proposed SDP4 (Lakeview Fields) zone was created for this area and is included in the
General Plan Appendix N / C-5. While the General Plan Map designates this area as mixed-use
broadly, the appendix provides more detailed information for the development of this specific
area. This area is identified, in the appendix, as a location that should be developed as a
Specific Development Plan. The Specific Development Plan 4 (SDP4) ordinance was created as
a result. The SDP4 zone has been applied to property along 2000 N. There is a parcel, just
north of this site, that has also recently applied for rezone to SDP4 too.
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                           *Item 1
June 24, 2026                                                                              Page 2

The preliminary plat originally showed 35 proposed lots and was reduced to 33 lots to comply
with minimum lot size standards of 8,500 square feet in the proposed zone. There is a proposed
stub street that would provide future access to the street network for the adjacent property to the
south. The property owner to the south has indicated that this stub street, that would potentially
benefit him in the future if he desired to subdivide his property, was not desired; so, this stub
may be removed with the final plat submittal.
Staff have reviewed the preliminary plat and found that it would be able to comply with minimum
lot standards and requirements with the proposed zone if approved.
Single-family subdivisions are an administrative approval by staff, therefore, there is no concept
plan or subdivision approval before the Planning Commission.

       Appendix N / C-5 in the General Plan shows that a resolution was adopted to amend the

POSSIBLE MOTIONS AND FINDINGS
The Planning Commission may make any of the following findings:

        1. Recommend Approval: Finding that the proposal is consistent with the General
           Plan. Move to recommend approval of the proposed zone map amendment from the
           R1.20 zone to the SDP4 zone to the Municipal Council.
        2. Recommend Denial: Finding that the proposal is not consistent with the General
           Plan. Move to recommend denial of the proposed zone map amendment from the
           R1.20 zone to the SDP4 zone to the Municipal Council.
        3. Continue: New information presented at the public hearing or additional study needs
           to be done. Move to continue the proposed zone map amendment from the R1.20
           zone to the SDP4 zone.

  ATTACHMENT 5 - NEIGHBORHOOD/DISTRICT MEETING MINUTES


Presentation on a Proposed Zone Change from R1.20 (One-Family
Residential) to SDP4 (Lakeview Fields) to Create a 35-Lot Single-Family
Subdivision at 1804 North Geneva Road in the Lakeview North
Neighborhood

Developer R. Jay Henrie presented a proposal to rezone an approximately 11-acre parcel
at 1804 North Geneva Road from R1.20 to SDP4 (Lakeview Fields) to develop a single-
family subdivision. Henrie noted that the property is co-owned with an adjacent parcel
owner and that he has resided in the general area for approximately 40 years.
Henrie explained that the Provo City General Plan from 2007 has long identified this area
for single-family residential development, consistent with the SDP4 zone that governs
neighboring subdivisions already under development. He noted that approximately three
and a half years ago, a prior proposal for twin-home development with a neighborhood
park and pickleball amenities was submitted to the Planning Commission and City Council
but was not approved. Since that time, Henrie stated that the project was delayed pending
utility infrastructure improvements, which have since been completed with assistance from
nearby development activity.
The current proposal, submitted to the city in late February, calls for 32 single-family lots,
reduced from an initial proposal of 35 lots in response to city feedback requiring
increased lot sizes. Henrie described the lots as generous in dimension, with most
approximately 84 to 85 feet wide and varying in depth. He indicated that the proposed lots
are comparable in size to those in adjacent phases of nearby development, and in some
cases slightly larger.
Henrie noted that the site plan has been designed to accommodate potential future access
for neighboring properties that may eventually subdivide, including stub roads to adjacent
parcels. He stated that a geotech study is nearing completion and that resubmission to the
city is anticipated within the following week. Henrie expressed hope that the project could
advance to the Planning Commission and City Council within the following one to two
months.
Resident concerns:
   • Twin homes or single-family homes? Henrie confirmed that the proposed homes are
      intended to be single-family detached residences—not twin homes as in the prior
      proposal.
   • Size and price of homes? Henrie indicated that his son, a general contractor,
      intends to build the homes, with plans targeting approximately 2,500 to 3,000
      square feet in size and an estimated price range around $700,000. He noted that
      basements are unlikely due to high water table conditions.
   •   Will there be an HOA? Henrie stated that one would only be formed if a
       detention basin requiring shared maintenance were required. He noted that
       an HOA is not otherwise planned.
   •   What is the SDP4 Zone? Planning staff was not at the meeting, but a West District
       Board Member stated that the SDP4 is a special zone established specifically for
       the Lakeview Fields area, running roughly from 1600 North to 2000 North, and that
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                       *Item 1
June 24, 2026                                                                         Page 10

       it includes a minimum lot size requirement of approximately 8,500 square feet.
Later answer from city planner: Provo City Code 14.49D creates the SDP-4 Lakeview
Fields Specific Development Plan Overlay Zone for the area east of Geneva Road
between 1680 North and 2000 North. It allows a mostly residential development with
single-family detached homes as the main housing type, plus a limited amount of multi-
family housing near 2000 North and Geneva Road. The code sets detailed rules for lot
size, width, setbacks, building height, parking, landscaping, fencing, architectural design,
detention basin maintenance, and homeowner/property owner association responsibilities.
It also limits the multi-family portion to 44 units, requires larger single-family homes with
two-car garages, and restricts occupancy to family occupancy, with multi-family units
limited to no more than three unrelated individuals.

City staff noted that the project is in the review phase and that the proposal presented may
differ from what is ultimately submitted to the Planning Commission and City Council.
Attendees were informed that public hearings will be scheduled and noticed via postcard to
nearby property owners, a white sign posted at the property, and the West District
webpage. Neighborhood Representatives were noted as expected to present at the
Planning Commission hearing as well.

NEIGHBORHOOD FEEDBACK
The North Timpview Neighborhood held a neighborhood meeting with the developer to provide
information about the proposal on June 1st, 2026. A report of that meeting and feedback
presented is attached to this staff report. Additionally, staff have received several emails prior
to this report that provide opinions and feedback on the proposal. Copies of these public
comments received prior to the report are attached to the rezone staff report (Item 3, June 24th,
2026, Planning Commission, PLRZ20260221).

PROJECT SUMMARY
The applicant, Mr. Bayles, proposes using the Regional Shopping Center (SC3) zone to
accomplish this vision. Information on the rezone request is contained in the rezone staff report
(Item 3, June 24, 2026, Planning Commission, PLRZ20260221).

This request is to amend the General Plan map to be in line with the proposed development
concept. Currently, the General Plan map designates the project area as agricultural and parks,
recreation, or open space. While some of the land would stay open for trails and natural open
space; the majority of the property would be improved for commercial uses. Therefore, the
applicant has proposed amending the General Plan to the commercial designation, which helps
to justify the requested zone change and concept plan (Items 3 and 4).

STAFF ANALYSIS
Before recommending an amendment, the Commission must find that the amendment serves
the public interest and aligns with the General Plan, based on consideration of the following:
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                             *Item 2
June 24, 2026                                                                                Page 2

      (a) Public purpose for the amendment in question;

      Applicant Response: “The current General Plan designation does not adequately
      reflect the type, scale, or public-facing character of the proposed project The requested
      amendment is needed because the site is being evaluated for a one-of-a-kind regional
      entertainment and hospitality use that requires a coordinated framework for assembly,
      access, infrastructure, and site organization.”

      Staff Response: Amending the General Plan map is needed to advance the proposed
      zone change and development plans as described by the applicant above.

      FINDING: Meets standard.


      (b) Confirmation that the public purpose is best served by the amendment in question;

      Applicant Response: “The site is uniquely situated for a gateway destination use. Its
      location at the mouth of Provo Canyon gives it regional visibility and access
      characteristics that are materially different from a typical infill or neighborhood property.”

      Staff Response: The current designation would allow the mining activity to continue,
      while the proposed amendment could facilitate what the applicant has described above.

      FINDING: Meets standard.


      (c) Compatibility of the proposed amendment with General Plan policies, goals, and
      objectives;

      Applicant Response: “The project will provide and enhance access to public trails and
      open space, which is consistent with current General Plan goals. The Provo Hillside and
      Canyons Plan, adopted December 12, 2023, recommended to “address the gravel pit
      reclamation” and “the creation of an outdoor concert venue.”

      Staff Response: The applicant has pointed to goals surrounding improved trails and
      open space, which are specifically noted in the following sections:
      - GP, chapter 8, goal 1a: Increase opportunities for recreation through development of
         passive spaces, active design, and educational opportunities.
      - GP, chapter 8, goal 2a: Create a strategic plan that includes tools and funding
         opportunities in order to conserve, connect, and protect vulnerable lands and open
         space.
      - GP, chapter 8, goal 4: Increase active and passive parks and recreational amenities.
      - Conservation and Resiliency Plan, chapter 3, Urban Nature goal 4: Increase and
         preserve open space as appropriate.
      Additionally, because the applicant mentions the Hillside and Canyons Plan specifically
      above, staff have found the following relevant sections in response:
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                          *Item 2
June 24, 2026                                                                             Page 3

      -   Strategy 1a.1.5: monitor and manage visitor use to ensure that recreational activities
          are sustainable and do not harm the environment. This may include limiting the
          number of visitors or restricting access during sensitive times.
      -   Strategy 2a.2.3: Increase the number of pavilions with tables. Consider the addition
          of larger facilities for events, such as large pavilion structures, amphitheaters, or
          educational centers.
      -   Strategy 2a.2.6: Consider the development of an event facility or amphitheater that
          would allow for concerts and other events at Provo Canyon.
      -   Implementation, page 69: Consider the creation of a task force to address gravel pit
          reclamation. This could include ecological restoration, the creation of an outdoor
          concert venue, or other recreation amenities.

      (d) Consistency of the proposed amendment with the General Plan’s “timing and
      sequencing” provisions on changes of use, insofar as they are articulated;

      Applicant Response: “The amendment better aligns the General Plan with the type of

      (e) Potential of the proposed amendment to hinder or obstruct attainment of the General
      Plan’s articulated policies;

      Applicant Response: No response.

      Staff Response: Staff are taking steps to protect against this, specifically with regard to

      development that could hinder or obstruct the attainment of General Plan policies and

      FINDING: Meets standard.

         ATTACHMENT 3 – NEIGHBORHOOD MEETING REPORT

  Resident Concerns re: proposed Vesper Amphitheater
  North Timpview Neighborhood Meeting - June 1, 2026

Overall Project Concerns

   •   Many residents opposed the project or expressed serious reservations about its
       size, location, and long-term impacts.

   •   Several speakers said the proposal appears too large and too fast-moving for such a
       sensitive canyon location.

   •   Residents repeatedly asked the City to slow down, complete studies ﬁrst, and allow
       more public review before any zoning change.

   •   Some questioned whether a 20,000-seat venue is appropriate at the mouth of Provo
       Canyon.

   •   Several comments challenged the framing that the only choices are leaving the
       gravel pit as-is or approving the Vesper proposal.

Traffic, Parking, and Public Safety

   •   Traffic was the most repeated concern.

   •   Residents questioned how 20,000 attendees could safely enter and exit through a
       constrained canyon corridor.

   •   Several people compared the situation to BYU game traffic, USANA Amphitheater
       traffic, and existing congestion during peak canyon use.

   •   Concerns included:

          o   One main road in and out of the canyon

          o   Congestion on Highway 189 and Canyon Road

          o   Neighborhood cut-through traffic

          o   Safety for pedestrians, bikers, runners, pets, and children

          o   Existing crashes and accident risks

          o   Emergency evacuation during wildﬁre or other disasters
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                       *Item 2
June 24, 2026                                                                          Page 9

   •   Many asked for detailed traffic studies, UDOT projections, ingress/egress plans, and
       mitigation strategies before approval.

   •   Parking concerns included whether on-site parking is realistic, whether shuttles
       would be used, how many vehicles would be accommodated, and whether parking
       for workers and guests would be sufficient.

Project Scale and Venue Size

   •   Many residents said the proposed venue is too large for the canyon.

   •   Several suggested a smaller venue, such as 5,000 seats or something closer in scale
       to Sundance, Tuacahn, Deer Valley, or Red Rocks.

   •   Some questioned why a 100-acre footprint is needed for a venue comparable in
       seating to the Marriott Center.

   •   Residents raised concerns about the amount of land, paving, parking, and
       infrastructure required for a 20,000-person venue.

   •   Some comments questioned whether the business model requires increasingly
       larger or more frequent events over time.

Zoning, Development Agreement, and Process

   •   Many residents asked that a formal development agreement be completed before
       any zone change is considered. Suggested development agreement components
       included:

          o   Clear allowed uses

          o   Deed restrictions

          o   Required studies

          o   Timelines and benchmarks

          o   Limits on event size and frequency

          o   Enforcement mechanisms

          o   Reversion to current zoning if the project becomes infeasible

   •   Several residents worried that once rezoning occurs, it may be difficult for the City to
       enforce commitments.

   •   Some comments said the process feels backwards and that impact studies should
       come before rezoning.
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                        *Item 2
June 24, 2026                                                                          Page 10

Environmental and Natural Resource Impacts

   •   Residents emphasized that Provo Canyon is a major natural and recreational asset
       for Utah Valley.

   •   Concerns included:

          o   Loss of open space

          o   Deforestation and heat from pavement

          o   Impacts to wildlife habitat

          o   Light pollution and dark-sky impacts

          o   Noise impacts

          o   Water use

          o   Groundwater, aquifers, springs, and ﬂood risk

          o   Construction impacts on nearby natural spaces

   •   Several residents said the canyon should be preserved as open space, not
       converted to commercial use.

   •   Some objected to describing the proposal as “restoration,” arguing that commercial
       development is not the same as restoring the gravel pit.

Alternatives to the Proposal

   •   Many residents asked the City to consider other options besides the Vesper project:

          o   Reclaiming the gravel pit as a natural recreation area

          o   Restoring wildlife habitat or watershed function

          o   Creating a public park

          o   Adding mountain bike trails

          o   Issuing a call for proposals for lower-impact reuse ideas

          o   Purchasing and restoring the gravel pit acreage

   •   Several residents argued that gravel pit reclamation does not require a large
       commercial development.

Open Space, Public Land, and Precedent
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                       *Item 2
June 24, 2026                                                                         Page 11

   •   Residents expressed support for the current open space preservation and
       recreation zoning.

   •   Several warned that approving commercial development at the canyon’s mouth
       could set a precedent for additional canyon development.

   •   Some raised concerns about public open space being used to support a private
       developer’s ﬁnancial model.

   •   Residents asked how public land, nearby natural areas, and conservation
       commitments would be protected long-term.

   •   Some urged that city-owned land retain its current open space zoning rather than
       being rezoned commercial with a later conservation easement.

Noise, Lighting, and Neighborhood Impacts

   •   Residents raised concerns about ampliﬁed sound, decibel levels, and compliance
       with Orem’s noise ordinance.

   •   Questions included what sound levels would be allowed at adjacent properties and
       what hours music would be permitted.

   •   Light pollution was raised as a concern for nearby residents, wildlife, and the night
       sky.

   •   Residents with experience living near stadiums or large venues described concerns
       about trespassing, property damage, crowd behavior, and neighborhood disruption.

Recreation and Trail Impacts

   •   Several speakers described the area as important for hiking, biking, equestrian use,
       and access to the Bonneville Shoreline Trail and Indian Road Trailhead.

   •   Concerns included increased foot traffic into adjacent hillside areas and insufficient
       controls to protect nearby trails and open space.

   •   Some residents said Provo Canyon is already heavily used and serves as a vital
       respite for surrounding communities.

Water, Infrastructure, and Costs

   •   Residents asked how the project would affect water resources, aquifers, springs,
       groundwater, and ﬂooding.

   •   Some questioned whether paving permeable land could increase runoff and ﬂood
       risk.
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                     *Item 2
June 24, 2026                                                                       Page 12

   •   Infrastructure questions included:

          o   Who would pay for sewer lines or other needed infrastructure?

          o   Whether capital funding is in place

          o   Whether the project can be ﬁnancially viable if it operates mainly seasonally

   •   Some residents questioned whether the scale of the project is being driven by the
       cost of land acquisition.

Supportive or Mixed Feedback

   •   A few residents offered more supportive or open-minded comments.

   •   Some said they want the gravel pit eliminated and asked opponents to identify a
       realistic alternative buyer or funding source.

   •   One resident said an amphitheater may be preferable to residential development
       because it would not be continuously occupied.

   •   Another urged residents to respect the private property rights process and allow the
       applicant to complete due diligence.

   •   Some supportive or mixed comments still suggested the venue should be smaller or
       subject to strict conditions.

Key Questions Raised by Residents

   •   Why does the venue need to be 20,000 seats?

   •   Why not consider a smaller venue?

   •   What is the realistic parking plan?

   •   How will traffic, ingress, and egress be handled?

   •   What are UDOT’s long-term traffic and crash projections?

   •   How would wildﬁre evacuation work with a large event underway?

   •   What studies will be required before approval?

   •   Why not complete the development agreement before rezoning?

   •   Can zoning revert if the project becomes infeasible?

   •   How will noise, light, water, wildlife, and trail impacts be mitigated?

   •   What other reuse or restoration options has the City considered?

             (Public Facilities), and OSPR (Open Space, Preservation, and Recreation) Zones
             to the SC3 (Regional Shopping Center) Zone for approximately 100.7 acres of
             land to allow for an entertainment and hospitality development, located at 6622 N

             PLRZ20260221


                      APPLICANT:            Bryan Bayles
                PROPERTY OWNER:             Provo Canyon Mining Co LLC; Provo City
                      PARCEL ID:            20:013:0001; 20:013:0016; 20:013:0018;
                                            20:013:0019; 20:013:0020
                  ACREAGE:                  100.7

  GENERAL PLAN DESIGNATION:                 Agricultural; Parks, Recreation, or Open Space
             CURRENT ZONE:                  Agricultural (A1.5); Public Facilities (PF); Open

NEIGHBORHOOD FEEDBACK
The North Timpview Neighborhood held a neighborhood meeting with the developer to provide
information about the proposal on June 1st, 2026. A summary of public feedback shared in that
meeting is attached to this staff report (neighborhood meeting report). Additionally, staff have
received numerous emails prior to this report that provide opinions and feedback on the
proposal. Copies of these public comments received prior to the report are also attached
(public comments - digital version only).

PROJECT SUMMARY
The applicant, Mr. Bayles, proposes using the Regional Shopping Center (SC3) zone to allow
for the development of a large amphitheater and hospitality project near the mouth of Provo
Canyon as shown in the concept site plan attached to this report and detailed in that staff report
for that item (Item 4, June 24, 2026, Planning Commission agenda). Early discussions with staff
raised concerns about many of the permitted uses allowed by-right within the SC3 zone to be
established at this site, so the developer has agreed to limit the uses on the property to just
those listed in the development agreement (a copy of the development agreement is attached
below). If approved as proffered in the agreement, the site would be limited to the following
uses:

   -   outdoor, open-air, partially enclosed, and enclosed venue operations for entertainment;
   -   amusement, public assemblies, filming and production, concert, conventions, artistic
       performances, theater, and other similar uses and activities;
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                              *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                                 Page 2

   -   plaza-based activities, guest-serving commercial and hospitality activities, and
       customary venue-support functions;
   -   a hotel of up to 75 rooms, restaurants, bars, cafes, food and beverage services, dining
       uses, and ancillary commercial and retail uses compatible with the primary amphitheater
       use;
   -   structured and underground parking, surface parking facilities, storage facilities, event-
       logistics areas, plazas, landscaped open space, and related uses and improvements;
       and
   -   temporary staging, event related tents (may not be used for overnight stays), festival-
       support and event-serving accessory uses, signage, and guest amenity uses customarily
       provided with venue, amphitheater, hotel, and food/beverage uses.

Other approvals and limitations proffered in the development agreement include details on
height standards, vested caps on proposed uses and parking, conservation easements for open
space areas, and additional developer obligations related to architectural theme/design, dark
sky lighting standards, utility infrastructure, noise, and access. All these obligations are listed in
sections 5-9 of the attached development agreement. It should also be noted that the
development agreement includes hard deadlines for approvals and construction, which if are not
met, would result in the reversion to prior zoning and remove any entitlements established by
the Vesper development.

STAFF ANALYSIS
Before recommending an amendment, the Commission must find that the amendment serves
the public interest and aligns with the General Plan, based on consideration of the following:

       (a) Public purpose for the amendment in question;

       Applicant Response: “The current PF / A-15 / OSP zoning does not adequately reflect
       the type, scale, or integrated character of the proposed project. The requested
       amendment is needed because the site is being evaluated for a one-of-a-kind regional
       entertainment and hospitality use that requires a coordinated framework for assembly,
       access, parking, infrastructure, and site organization. A more appropriate zoning
       designation is needed to capture that integrated use pattern, to bring the zoning in line
       with the vision set forth in the General Plan’s Hillside and Canyons Plan, and to provide
       a workable regulatory framework for the project.”

       Staff Response: The applicant is correct in that the desired purpose for the property
       would require a zone map amendment.

       FINDING: Meets standard.


       (b) Confirmation that the public purpose is best served by the amendment in question;

       Applicant Response: “SC3 is the best fit within Provo’s existing framework because it
       is specifically intended for commercial and service uses that serve an entire region, are
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                          *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                             Page 3

      located near freeways and major arterials, and function as an integrated architectural
      unit. That framework aligns well with the Vesper project as a coordinated, region-serving
      hospitality destination at the gateway to Provo Canyon.
      SC3 is also more appropriate because the zone already contemplates hospitality and
      destination-serving commercial uses. The project’s amphitheater, hotel, restaurants,
      commercial uses, parking, and support facilities are intended to function together as one
      coordinated destination rather than as scattered unrelated uses. The project is not being
      advanced as piecemeal strip commercial development. It is being advanced as a single
      integrated district with one overall concept, one circulation structure, and one
      coordinated identity.
      The physical and organizational character of the project is also consistent with the
      structure of SC3. The zone is intended for large-scale integrated development rather
      than a small freestanding parcel. That makes it a more appropriate existing zoning tool
      than narrower commercial districts that do not as naturally accommodate a project of this
      size, complexity, and regional draw.
      SC3 is further justified because it gives the City a familiar and workable regulatory
      structure while preserving the ability to tailor the final approval package. The intent for
      the project is to use a commercially and publicly legible base zone while still allowing the
      City to shape the final list of uses, design commitments, and project-specific obligations
      through the Development Agreement process.”

      Staff Response: After reviewing available zones in Provo, the SC3 zone was confirmed
      as the best fit for the overall concept, subject to certain limitations captured in the
      proposed development agreement.

      FINDING: Meets standard.


      (c) Compatibility of the proposed amendment with General Plan policies, goals, and
      objectives;

      Applicant Response: “Provo Hillside and Canyons Plan, adopted December 12, 2023,
      identifies this land for an “outdoor concert venue” [and] the amendment is being
      requested in a coordinated entitlement package with a General Plan Map Amendment
      and a Concept Plan.”

      Staff Response: Relevant goals and policies from the Hillside and Canyons Plan
      specifically mentioned above include (other relevant General Plan goals can be found in
      the associated General Plan Map amendment staff report - item #2, June 24, 2026,
      Planning Commission agenda):
      - Strategy 1a.1.5: monitor and manage visitor use to ensure that recreational activities
          are sustainable and do not harm the environment. This may include limiting the
          number of visitors or restricting access during sensitive times.
      - Strategy 2a.2.3: Increase the number of pavilions with tables. Consider the addition
          of larger facilities for events, such as large pavilion structures, amphitheaters, or
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                          *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                             Page 4

          educational centers.
      -   Strategy 2a.2.6: Consider the development of an event facility or amphitheater that
          would allow for concerts and other events at Provo Canyon.
      -   Implementation, page 69: Consider the creation of a task force to address gravel pit
          reclamation. This could include ecological restoration, the creation of an outdoor
          concert venue, or other recreation amenities.

      FINDING: Primarily Compatible.


      (d) Consistency of the proposed amendment with the General Plan’s “timing and
      sequencing” provisions on changes of use, insofar as they are articulated;

      Applicant Response: “The Concept Plan is intended to identify the project’s principal
      development envelopes while preserving appropriate flexibility for exact layout
      refinements as engineering, access authorization, utility planning, and feasibility work
      continue.”

      Staff Response: While there are insufficient services to this property, the development
      agreement under review commits the developer to provide, at their cost, the required
      infrastructure for the proposed development.

      FINDING: Does not currently meet standard.


      (e) Potential of the proposed amendment to hinder or obstruct attainment of the General
      Plan’s articulated policies;

      Applicant Response: No response.

      Staff Response: Staff are taking steps to protect against this, specifically with regard to
      limits of the development, timing of improvements, and reversion clauses in the
      development agreement of three years to commence construction and seven years for
      project completion. The development agreement should protect against unanticipated
      development that could hinder or obstruct the attainment of General Plan policies and
      goals.

      FINDING: Meets standard.


      (f) Adverse impacts on adjacent landowners; and

      Applicant Response: No response.

      Staff Response: There will be impacts to surrounding landowners with new noise, light,
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                             *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                                Page 5

       and traffic. The city will protect against these impacts as much as possible if the
       proposals are approved by enforcing noise and light regulations in code and requiring a
       traffic study to highlight required improvements. There are additional safeguards from
       these impacts provided in the attached development agreement.

       FINDING: Meets standard.


       (g) Verification of correctness in the original zoning or General Plan for the area in
       question.

       Applicant Response: No response.

       Staff Response: Staff have verified the zone and general plan maps are correct.

       FINDING: Meets standard.


       (h) In cases where a conflict arises between the General Plan Map and General Plan
       Policies, precedence shall be given to the Plan Policies.

       Applicant Response: No response.

       Staff Response: Policies have been given precedence, and the map is proposed to be
       amended.

       FINDING: Meets standard.


POSSIBLE MOTIONS AND FINDINGS
The Planning Commission may make any of the following findings (state findings):

       1. Recommend Approval: The proposal is consistent with the General Plan policies.
          (Planning Commission recommends approval to the City Council of a Zone Map
          Amendment from the A1, PF, and OSPR Zones to the SC3 Zone for 100.7 acres of
          land located at 6622 N Hwy 189, subject to approval of a development agreement)
       2. Recommend Denial: The proposal is not consistent with the General Plan policies.
          (Planning Commission recommends denial to the City Council of a Zone Map
          Amendment from the A1, PF, and OSPR Zones to the SC3 Zone for 100.7 acres of
          land located at 6622 N Hwy 189)
       3. Continue: New information presented at the public hearing or additional study needs to be
          done. (The Planning Commission continues this item (state reasons).

         ATTACHMENT 3 – NEIGHBORHOOD MEETING REPORT

  Resident Concerns re: proposed Vesper Amphitheater
  North Timpview Neighborhood Meeting - June 1, 2026

Overall Project Concerns

   •   Many residents opposed the project or expressed serious reservations about its
       size, location, and long-term impacts.

   •   Several speakers said the proposal appears too large and too fast-moving for such a
       sensitive canyon location.

   •   Residents repeatedly asked the City to slow down, complete studies ﬁrst, and allow
       more public review before any zoning change.

   •   Some questioned whether a 20,000-seat venue is appropriate at the mouth of Provo
       Canyon.

   •   Several comments challenged the framing that the only choices are leaving the
       gravel pit as-is or approving the Vesper proposal.

Traffic, Parking, and Public Safety

   •   Traffic was the most repeated concern.

   •   Residents questioned how 20,000 attendees could safely enter and exit through a
       constrained canyon corridor.

   •   Several people compared the situation to BYU game traffic, USANA Amphitheater
       traffic, and existing congestion during peak canyon use.

   •   Concerns included:

          o   One main road in and out of the canyon

          o   Congestion on Highway 189 and Canyon Road

          o   Neighborhood cut-through traffic

          o   Safety for pedestrians, bikers, runners, pets, and children

          o   Existing crashes and accident risks

          o   Emergency evacuation during wildﬁre or other disasters
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                       *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                         Page 10

   •   Many asked for detailed traffic studies, UDOT projections, ingress/egress plans, and
       mitigation strategies before approval.

   •   Parking concerns included whether on-site parking is realistic, whether shuttles
       would be used, how many vehicles would be accommodated, and whether parking
       for workers and guests would be sufficient.

Project Scale and Venue Size

   •   Many residents said the proposed venue is too large for the canyon.

   •   Several suggested a smaller venue, such as 5,000 seats or something closer in scale
       to Sundance, Tuacahn, Deer Valley, or Red Rocks.

   •   Some questioned why a 100-acre footprint is needed for a venue comparable in
       seating to the Marriott Center.

   •   Residents raised concerns about the amount of land, paving, parking, and
       infrastructure required for a 20,000-person venue.

   •   Some comments questioned whether the business model requires increasingly
       larger or more frequent events over time.

Zoning, Development Agreement, and Process

   •   Many residents asked that a formal development agreement be completed before
       any zone change is considered. Suggested development agreement components
       included:

          o   Clear allowed uses

          o   Deed restrictions

          o   Required studies

          o   Timelines and benchmarks

          o   Limits on event size and frequency

          o   Enforcement mechanisms

          o   Reversion to current zoning if the project becomes infeasible

   •   Several residents worried that once rezoning occurs, it may be difficult for the City to
       enforce commitments.

   •   Some comments said the process feels backwards and that impact studies should
       come before rezoning.
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                        *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                          Page 11

   •   Residents emphasized that Provo Canyon is a major natural and recreational asset
       for Utah Valley.

          o   Loss of open space

          o   Deforestation and heat from pavement

          o   Impacts to wildlife habitat

          o   Light pollution and dark-sky impacts

          o   Noise impacts

          o   Water use

          o   Groundwater, aquifers, springs, and ﬂood risk

          o   Construction impacts on nearby natural spaces

   •   Several residents said the canyon should be preserved as open space, not
       converted to commercial use.

   •   Some objected to describing the proposal as “restoration,” arguing that commercial
       development is not the same as restoring the gravel pit.

Alternatives to the Proposal

   •   Many residents asked the City to consider other options besides the Vesper project:

          o   Reclaiming the gravel pit as a natural recreation area

          o   Restoring wildlife habitat or watershed function

          o   Creating a public park

          o   Adding mountain bike trails

          o   Issuing a call for proposals for lower-impact reuse ideas

          o   Purchasing and restoring the gravel pit acreage

   •   Several residents argued that gravel pit reclamation does not require a large
       commercial development.

                             (Provo Canyon Amphitheater Venue)


        THIS DEVELOPMENT AGREEMENT FOR VESPER (this “Agreement”) is made and
entered into as of the _____ day of ____________, 2026 (the “Effective Date”), by and between
the CITY OF PROVO, a Utah municipal corporation, hereinafter referred to as “City,” and
VESPER PROPERTY COMPANY, LLC, a Utah limited liability company, hereinafter referred
to as “Developer.” The City and Developer are hereinafter collectively referred to as “Parties.”

                                          RECITALS

        A.     Developer holds the contractual rights to purchase certain land located within the
City of Provo more particularly described on Exhibit A, attached hereto and incorporated herein
by reference (the “Property”) and to develop a portion of that land as discussed below.

       B.      On ______________, 2026, the City Council approved Ordinance ____________
(the “Vesting Ordinance”), based on the Concept Plan set forth on Exhibit B (the “Concept
Plan”), attached hereto and incorporated herein by reference. The Concept Plan will govern the
density, development, and use of the Property (said density, development, and use constituting
the “Project”).

        C.      Developer is willing to design and construct the Project in a manner that is in
harmony with and intended to promote the long range policies, goals, and objectives of the
City’s general plan, zoning, and development regulations in order to receive the benefit of
vesting for certain uses and zoning designations under the terms of this Agreement as more fully
set forth below.

       D.       The City Council accepted Developer’s proffer to enter into this Agreement to
memorialize the intent of Developer and City and decreed that the effective date of the Vesting
Ordinance be the date of the execution and delivery of this Agreement and the recording thereof
as a public record on title of the Property in the office of the Utah County Recorder.

       E.     The City Council further authorized the Mayor of the City to execute and deliver
this Agreement on behalf of the City.

       F.     The City has the authority to enter into this Agreement pursuant to Utah Code
Section 10-20-508 (2026) and relevant municipal ordinances, and desires to enter into this
Agreement with the Developer for the purpose of guiding the development of the Property in
accordance with the terms and conditions of this Agreement and in accordance with applicable
City Ordinances.
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                           *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                             Page 16

        G.     This Agreement is consistent with, and all preliminary and Concept Plans within
the Property are subject to and must conform with, the City’s General Plan, Zoning Ordinances,
and Subdivision Ordinances, and any permits issued by the City pursuant to City Ordinances and
regulations.

        H.      The Parties desire to enter into this Agreement to specify the rights and
responsibilities of the Developer to develop the Property as expressed in this Agreement and the
rights and responsibilities of the City to allow and regulate such development pursuant to the
requirements of this Agreement.

      I.      The Parties understand and intend that this Agreement is a “development
agreement” within the meaning of, and entered into pursuant to, the terms of Utah Code Ann.,
§10-20-508.

       J.      The Parties intend to be bound by the terms of this Agreement as set forth herein.

                                         AGREEMENT

       NOW, THEREFORE, in consideration of the mutual covenants contained herein, and
other good and valuable consideration, the receipt and sufficiency of which is hereby
acknowledged, the City and the Developer hereby agree as follows:

      1.      Incorporation of Recitals. The foregoing Recitals are hereby incorporated into this
Agreement, as a substantive part hereof.

        2.     Zoning. Approval of any portion of this Agreement that grants exceptions to
current City code must follow the same procedures for enacting a land use regulation under Utah
Code Ann. § 10-20-502. The terms of this Agreement are enforceable and the rights of
Developer to develop the Property in accordance with this Agreement vests only if the Provo
City Council, in the independent exercise of its legislative authority, approves a zoning change of
the Property, or any portion thereof, to the SC3, Regional Shopping Center Zone, and approves
this Agreement. The requested zoning change and this Agreement will be processed and
reviewed concurrently by the City. If the City grants such legislative approvals, then the City and
Developer will execute this Agreement, and development of the Property will be governed by
this Agreement, the approved Concept Plan, and applicable provisions of the City Code.

        3.      Governing Standards. The Concept Plan, the Vesting Ordinance and this
Agreement establish the development rights for the Project, including the permitted use,
maximum densities, intensity and general configuration for the Project. The Project must be
developed by the Developer in accordance with the Concept Plan, the Vesting Ordinance and
this Agreement. All Developer submittals must comply generally with the Concept Plan, the
Vesting Ordinance and this Agreement. Non-material variations to the Concept Plan may be
approved by the City’s Development Services Director without City Council or Planning
Commission approval. For purposes of this provision, “non-material” variations include
alterations to building footprint locations and pedestrian walkways, adjustments to accommodate
engineering specifications, locations of open space and parking, updated architectural designs
and features of similar quality, and other such modifications so long as such variations do not
materially change the permitted uses or the overall design and development standards of the
Project.

                      iii. plaza-based activities, guest-serving commercial and hospitality
                           activities, and customary venue-support functions;

                      iv. a hotel of up to 75 rooms, restaurants, bars, cafes, food and beverage
                          services, dining uses, and ancillary commercial and retail uses
                          compatible with the primary amphitheater use;

                       v. structured and underground parking, surface parking facilities, storage
                          facilities, event-logistics areas, plazas, landscaped open space, and
                          related uses and improvements; and

                      vi. temporary staging, event related tents (may not be used for overnight
                          stays), festival-support and event-serving accessory uses, signage, and
                          guest amenity uses customarily provided with venue, amphitheater,
                          hotel, and food/beverage uses.

        5.     Height Standards. Structures in the Project must comply with applicable SC3
height standards, except as provided in this section. However, the Parties agree that specialized,
non-occupied stage-support and roof-related elements, including theatrical rigging, fly-loft, set-
operation, and roof/sightline elements may exceed the SC3 height restrictions, but may not
exceed a height of 125 feet.

       6.      Vested Caps. The amphitheater capacity may include up to 20,000 seats.
Commercial uses including retail and operations may include up to 150,000 square feet in the
aggregate. The Project must include an adequate number of parking stalls to serve the Project
and to meet City Code requirements, but in no event more than 8,000 parking stalls in the
aggregate at the Property.

         7.     Conservation Easement on Undeveloped Property. If the City Council, in its
legislative discretion, approves this Agreement and rezones the Property, then the entirety of the
Property will be rezoned to the SC3 zone. In connection with the final Project approvals, those
portions of the Property that will not be developed for the uses described in Section 4 above (the
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                          *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                            Page 18

“Undeveloped Property”) will be clearly delineated. The City shall then have the unilateral right
to rezone the Undeveloped Property to the OSPR zone, and Developer hereby consents to the
same. Furthermore, as part of the Project, the Developer must do the following:

               a. Improve the Undeveloped Property in accordance with the approved
                  construction plans for the Project, including revegetation and trail
                  improvement and creation; and

               b. Upon completion of the improvements described in Section 7(a), place the
                  Undeveloped Property in a conservation easement that is held by a third party
                  that specializes in the preservation of open space and that prohibits
                  commercial, industrial, or residential development of the Undeveloped
                  Property. The conservation easement shall allow the owner/operator of the
                  Project to implement appropriate measures and safety protocols to protect the
                  venue and other improvements that are developed within the acreage rezoned
                  to the SC3 zone. The conservation easement shall also allow for the use and
                  maintenance of public trails, as approved by the City.

       8.      Additional Specific Developer Obligations. As an integral part of the
consideration for this Agreement, the Developer voluntarily agrees as follows:

               a. The Project must be designed and constructed in a manner generally
                  consistent with a high-quality canyon-integrated destination environment.
                  Natural materials and natural design elements must be substantially
                  incorporated into the finished Project, including meaningful use of materials
                  such as wood, stone, glass, and other materials of similar quality and
                  character. Buildings, plazas, open spaces, and support areas must be designed
                  to respond to the site’s topography, views, and mountain setting. Design
                  expression, architectural treatment, and material allocation may evolve
                  through later design development. No provision of this Section may be
                  interpreted to require any fixed architectural style or to prevent reasonable
                  changes in final design so long as the Project remains generally consistent
                  with the character standards set forth herein.

               b. Project lighting must be designed to be Dark Sky compliant to the extent
                  reasonably possible. Signage and wayfinding must be limited in scale and
                  character and must be designed as a coordinated, canyon-sensitive system
                  generally consistent with the Project and its natural setting. Such signage and
                  wayfinding may include reasonable project identification, directional,
                  parking, pedestrian, trail, safety, and event-operation signage customarily
                  needed to support the approved Project, provided that the final signage
                  program remains visually compatible with the canyon environment and does
                  not materially detract from the surrounding natural setting.

               c. Maintenance, loading, storage, and back-of-house areas must be screened or
                  otherwise designed so as to reasonably minimize visual impacts from public
                  areas and surrounding viewpoints.
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                            *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                              Page 19

               d. Structured parking and related support improvements must be integrated into
                  the site topography and visually softened through design, grading,
                  landscaping, screening, or a combination thereof, to the extent commercially
                  reasonable and feasible.

               e. Developer must comply with all applicable standards and requirements for
                  utility services and coordination, infrastructure layout, and access (ingress and
                  egress) for vehicles and pedestrians. Pedestrian trails must be designed and
                  installed in a manner designed to protect pedestrian safety and minimize
                  interface with vehicular traffic.

               f. Developer must comply with applicable standards and requirements for sewer
                  connection, culinary and fire-flow service, stormwater planning, and power-
                  service planning. Any City infrastructure, or County infrastructure connected
                  to City infrastructure, damaged or disrupted by construction by Developer of
                  infrastructure required for the Project, must be repaired or replaced to the
                  satisfaction of the City at Developer’s cost. For example, any roadways or
                  trails excavated in order to install utilities must be repaired or replaced as
                  indicated in this paragraph.

               g. Developer must use commercially reasonable efforts to incorporate current
                  and evolving sound-attenuation and noise-mitigation technologies into the
                  design and operation of the Project in order to reduce off-site sound and noise
                  impacts to the extent reasonably practicable.

               h. Unless specifically designated otherwise in the final Project approvals, all
                  roads in the Project are private roads and the owner or operator of the
                  Property is responsible for all maintenance and snow plowing for such roads.
                  If, subject to this paragraph, it is decided that any road in the Project will be
                  public, the owner or operator of the Project is still responsible for snow
                  plowing of that road.

        9.      City Obligations. As an integral part of the consideration of this Agreement and
the success of the Project, the City agrees as follows:

               a. To the extent that the final Project approvals require Developer to construct or
                  install improvements or facilities that benefit or provide services to other
                  properties (such as a water tank and water service lines the provide water to
                  other property(ies), potential roadway connections to other development(s), or
                  sewer facilities that service other property(ies), Developer will front the
                  payment of the costs of such improvement but shall have the right to be
                  reimbursed for the share of such costs that are proportionately allocated to the
                  other property(ies) that benefit from the improvements for a period of ten
                  years after the costs are incurred. The City will cooperate in good faith with
                  Developer to facilitate appropriate reimbursements to Developer through cost-
                  sharing agreements, impact fee credits, or other reimbursement mechanisms
                  consistent with Utah law. After the ten-year period mentioned above,
                  Developer will not be entitled to any more reimbursement or credits.

             b. The City shall not require Developer to upsize any future public infrastructure

             c. The City will cooperate with Developer in preparing, reviewing and
                implementing event-specific traffic management plans for the Project and its
                events. The City will make good faith efforts to support traffic management
                with services from the City’s police department; provided, however, that

                    ii. public infrastructure districts and similar district-based financing

                         assessment-based financing structures;

                    iv. reimbursement agreements, reimbursement districts, cost-sharing
                        arrangements, and other lawful repayment or recapture mechanisms

                    vi. county tourism funds.

             For clarification, each of the above-referenced financing mechanisms will be

              policy-making authority, elects to approve one or more of these financing and

              Following approval of the Project, the City and Developer will work in good faith

         10.   Grading and Site Adaptation. Substantial portions of the Property consist of
previously mined and disturbed gravel-pit terrain rather than intact undisturbed hillside.
Construction and final site configuration for the Project may therefore require continued
refinement of grading, retaining, drainage, revegetation, pad creation, parking layout, utility
placement, and related site-adaptation measures. Such refinements and grading activities are
permitted within the vested framework established by this Agreement in order to prepare the site
for the intended development of the Project. Developer must comply with the City’s hillside
development ordinances and may not disturb any slope greater than 30% that has not been
previously disturbed without first obtaining approval through the applicable City variance
process as part of project plan approval before the Planning Commission.

       11.    Vested Rights and Reserved Legislative Powers.

               a. Vested Rights. As of the Effective Date, subject to the terms and provisions of
                  this Agreement, Developer has the vested right to develop and construct the
                  Project in accordance with the uses, maximum caps, intensities, and general
                  configuration of development established in the Concept Plan, as
                  supplemented by the Vesting Ordinance and this Agreement (and all
                  Exhibits), subject to compliance with the City Ordinances in existence on the
                  Effective Date. The Parties intend that the rights granted to Developer under
                  this Agreement are contractual and also those rights that exist under statute,
                  common law and at equity. The Parties specifically intend that this Agreement
                  grants to Developer “vested rights” as that term is construed in Utah’s
                  common law and pursuant to Utah Code Ann., §10-20-101 et seq. Moreover,
                  in the event of any conflict between the terms of this Agreement and the
                  general provisions of the City Code, the terms of this Agreement control as
                  provided in Utah Code Ann. § 10-20-508(2)(a)(iii).

                       i. Examples of Exceptions to Vested Rights. The Parties understand and
                          agree that the Project will be required to comply with future changes
                          to City Laws that do not limit or interfere with the vested rights
                          granted pursuant to the terms of this Agreement. The following are
                          examples for illustrative purposes of a non-exhaustive list of the type
                          of future laws that may be enacted by the City that would be
                          applicable to the Project:

                             1. Developer Agreement. Future laws that Developer agrees in

                             3. Safety Code Updates. Future laws that are updates or

               b. Reserved Legislative Powers. The Developer acknowledges that the City is
                  restricted in its authority to limit its police power by contract and that the
                  limitations, reservations and exceptions set forth herein are intended to
                  reserve to the City all of its police power that cannot be so limited.
                  Notwithstanding the retained power of the City to enact such legislation of the

                  as set forth herein unless facts and circumstances are present that meet the
                  exceptions to the vested rights doctrine as set forth in Section 10-20-101 et

       12.    Default. An “Event of Default” occurs under this Agreement if any party fails to
perform a material obligation expressly required hereunder when due and the defaulting party
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                            *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                              Page 23

has not performed or cured the delinquent obligations within sixty (60) days following delivery
to the delinquent party of written notice of such delinquency describing the alleged default in
reasonable detail. Notwithstanding the foregoing, if the default cannot reasonably be cured
within that 60-day period, a party is not in default so long as that party commences to cure the
default within that 60-day period and diligently continues such cure in good faith until complete.

               a.     Remedies. Upon the occurrence of an uncured Event of Default, the non-
       defaulting party has the right to exercise the following rights and remedies against the
       defaulting party:

                       1.      All rights and remedies available at law and in equity, including
               injunctive relief, specific performance, and termination, but not including
               damages or attorney’s fees; provided, however, that termination is available only
               for a material uncured Event of Default that substantially frustrates the essential
               purpose of this agreement as to the affected portion of the Property or Project.

                       2.      The right to withhold further approvals, licenses, permits or other
               rights associated with the Project or development activity pertaining to the
               defaulting party as described in this Agreement until such default has been cured;
               provided, however, that any such withholding must be reasonably and directly
               related to the specific obligation that remains in uncured default and may not be
               used to withhold, delay, or impair unrelated approvals, licenses, permits,
               inspections, or rights associated with other portions of the Project.

                       3.      The right to draw upon any security posted or provided in
               connection with the Property or Project by the defaulting party; provided,
               however, that any such draw is limited to security specifically posted to secure the
               obligation that remains in uncured default and is limited to the amount reasonably
               necessary to cure such default.

The rights and remedies set forth herein are cumulative, subject to the limitations expressly set
forth in this Section.

       14.   General Term and Conditions.

             a. Headings. The headings contained in this Agreement are intended for
                convenience only and are in no way to be used to construe or limit the text
                herein.

             b. Property Acquisition Contingency. Notwithstanding any other provision to
                the contrary in this Agreement, this Agreement does not take effect unless and
                until Developer (or an entity owned or controlled by Developer) acquires title
                to the Property within one (1) year after the date of approval of this
                Agreement by the Provo City Council. This Agreement will not be recorded
                against the Property with the Utah County Recorder’s Office unless and until
                this acquisition contingency has been satisfied.

             c. Binding Effect. Subject to the other provisions hereof, this Agreement inures
                to the benefit of, and be binding upon, the parties hereto and their respective
                heirs, representatives, officers, agents, employees, members, successors and
                assigns (to the extent that assignment is permitted). Without limiting the
                generality of the foregoing, a “successor” includes a party that succeeds to the
                rights and interests of the Developer as evidenced by, among other things,
                such party’s submission of land use applications to the City relating to the
                Property or the Project.

             a. Non Liability of City Officials and Employees. No officer, representative,
                consultant, attorney, agent or employee of the City is personally liable to the
                Developer, or any successor in interest or assignee of the Developer, for any
                default or breach by the City, or for any amount that may become due to the
                Developer, or its successors or assignees, or for any obligation arising under
                the terms of this Agreement. Nothing herein will release any person from
                personal liability for their own individual acts or omissions.

             b. Third Party Rights. Except for the Developer, the City and other parties that
                may succeed the Developer on title to any portion of the Property, all of
                whom are express intended beneficiaries of this Agreement, this Agreement
                does not create any rights in and/or obligations to any other persons or parties.
                The Parties acknowledge that this Agreement refers to a private development
                and that the City has no interest in, responsibility for, or duty to any third
                parties concerning any improvements to the Property unless the City has
                accepted the dedication of such improvements

             c. Further Documentation. This Agreement is entered into by the Parties with
                the recognition and anticipation that subsequent agreements, plans, profiles,
                engineering and other documentation implementing and carrying out the
                provisions of this Agreement may be necessary. The Parties agree to negotiate
                and act in good faith with respect to all such future items.

June 24, 2026                                                                           Page 25

             d. Relationship of Parties. This Agreement does not create any joint venture,
                partnership, undertaking, business arrangement or fiduciary relationship
                between the City and the Developer.

             e. Agreement to Run With the Land. This Agreement must be recorded in the
                Office of the Utah County Recorder against the Property and is intended to
                and is deemed to run with the land, and is binding on and benefits all
                successors in the ownership of any portion of the Property.

             f. Performance. Each party, person and/or entity governed by this Agreement
                must perform its respective obligations under this Agreement in a manner that
                will not unreasonably or materially delay, disrupt or inconvenience any other
                party, person and/or entity governed by this Agreement, the development of
                any portion of the Property or the issuance of Concept Plans, certificates of
                occupancy or other approvals associated therewith.

             g. Applicable Law. This Agreement is entered into under and pursuant to, and is
                to be construed and enforceable in accordance with, the laws of the State of
                Utah.

             h. Construction. This Agreement has been reviewed and revised by legal counsel
                for both the City and the Developer, and no presumption or rule that
                ambiguities is construed against the drafting party applies to the interpretation
                or enforcement of this Agreement.

             i. Consents and Approvals. Except as expressly stated in this Agreement, the
                consent, approval, permit, license or other authorization of any party under
                this Agreement must be given in a prompt and timely manner and may not be
                unreasonably withheld, conditioned or delayed. Any consent, approval,
                permit, license or other authorization required hereunder from the City must
                be given or withheld by the City in compliance with this Agreement and the
                City Ordinances.

             j. Approval and Authority to Execute. Each of the Parties represents and
                warrants as of the Effective Date this Agreement, it/he/she has all requisite
                power and authority to execute and deliver this Agreement, being fully
                authorized so to do and that this Agreement constitutes a valid and binding
                agreement.

             k. Deadlines.

                    a. Developer has a period of up to three (3) years following final
                       approval of the Project to commence construction. Delay in
                       commencement during such two-year period does not constitute a
                       default, abandonment, lapse, forfeiture, or loss of rights under this

                         approval of the Project to achieve Substantial Completion. During that
                         period, lack of Substation Completion does not constitute a default,
                         abandonment, lapse, forfeiture, or loss of rights under this Agreement,

                         Developer continues in good faith toward Project completion. For
                         purposes of this Agreement, “Substantial Completion” means that the

              m. Termination.

                     i.       Notwithstanding anything in this Agreement to the contrary, it is
              agreed by the parties hereto that in the event the Concept Plan for the Property has
              not been recorded in the Office of the Utah County Recorder within ten (10) years
              from the date of this Agreement (the “Term”), or upon the occurrence of an event
              of default of this Agreement that is not cured, the City has the right, but not the
              obligation, at the sole discretion of the City Council, to terminate this Agreement
              as to the defaulting party (i.e., the Developer). The Term may be extended by
              mutual agreement of the Parties.

                    ii.       Upon termination of this Agreement for the reasons set forth
              herein, following the notice and process required hereby, the obligations of the
              City and the defaulting party to each other hereunder terminate, but none of the
              licenses, building permits, or certificates of occupancy granted prior to expiration
              of the Term or termination of this Agreement may be rescinded or limited in any
              manner.

               a. Notice. Developer must give Notice to the City of any proposed assignment

in writing by the party intended to be benefited by the provisions, and a waiver by a party of a
breach hereunder by the other party may not be construed as a waiver of any succeeding breach
of the same or other provisions.

reason, the remaining provisions continue in full force and effect.
