                      ATTACHMENT 5 – PUBLIC COMMENTS

                  Vesper Public Comments (May 13 – June 16)
Eric Luna – May 13

I received the email below from Conserve Utah Valley.

I imagine there will be opposition to the Vesper Amphitheater Rezone & Concept & General
Plan Amendment; there is for every project these days.

It looks like a wonderful project that will enhance the quality of life for area residents and
eliminate a blight, so I wanted to voice my personal support for the project. I am not speaking for
either group I run, as neither has taken a position on the project.

Thank you.

I have gathered a few emails of fishermen and women concerned with protecting Provo River
and Canyon. The following is a statement from my organization, Conserve Utah Valley, about
an application for a change in zoning for land inside the canyon on the lower Provo River. We
are educating people so they will come to the Provo North Division meeting on July 29. Feel
free to forward this information to anyone interested:

Earlier this week a few Conserve Utah Valley board members met with Provo Mayor Marsha
Judkins and a developer, Spencer Shumway. His partnership has requested the rezoning of 101
acres of land just inside the mouth of Provo Canyon. Forty acres of the land is currently owned
by Steve Turley, who operates a gravel pit there. The planned resort would include a 20,000
seat concert amphitheater and a cluster of hotels and shops. Most of the parking would be
underground to the west of the resort. The developer touted having access to trails for bikes and
hikers. He said the benches would be wooden and the pathways would be rock and it would be
a lovely natural area. He said that it would only be used 10 times a year for the full 20,000 seats
and 120 times a year total, however, there would be a smaller amount of daily traffic for patrons
of the hotels, eateries and shops.

In our meeting, we talked about what Provo Canyon is known for—outdoor recreation. It is a
Blue Ribbon Flyfishing river, and the Provo and Jordan River Parkway Trail follows the river. We
have hikers, walkers, bikers, skateboarders, e-bike riders, cross country and down-hill skiers,
snowshoers, and horse riders. It has half a dozen parks where people camp and picnic. The
river also gives pleasure to rafters, tubers, and kayakers. Part of the pleasure is the possibility of
seeing wildlife. We owe it to the people who enjoy the canyon to take supreme care before we
undertake any development that threatens the known identity of the canyon. We also talked with
the developer about traffic and sound, light, and vehicle pollution. He said they would mitigate
these problems by having subdued lighting and a modern sound system.

Below find the announcement of the application for rezoning. We’ve also attached t material
from the developer. The developer will tentatively make a presentation at the Provo District
meeting on 29 July 2026. Meetings start at 6:00 PM at City Hall and over Zoom. This will also
go before the Planning Commission and City Council (dates not yet scheduled.)
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                              *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                                Page 35



Joy Patten – May 20

Dear Provo City Council Members,

I live in the neighborhood at the mouth of Provo Canyon. I have a couple of questions about the
Vesper Amphitheater project in Provo Canyon that I am hoping you will address.

First, I am hoping the Council has requested sound studies to be done. I live right next to
Palisade Park. The noise level in our neighborhood has grown significantly over the last 10
years due to the park, two helicopters and the traffic along 800 north. How will the sound of the
Amphitheater affect our neighborhood? Am I going to be listening to concerts that I didn’t
choose to attend?

Second, the traffic at the mouth of the canyon can become very congested, particularly at peak
travel times. I’ve also witnessed a multitude of accidents at the mouth of the canyon and as
from people entering traffic on Provo Canyon Road from the canyon parks and South Fork.
How does the developer plan to mitigate traffic congestion and the potential for accidents due to
the considerable increase in traffic this project will create near the mouth of the canyon?

I respectfully request you address these questions prior to approving this project.

Respectfully,

Joy Patten



Jared Toone – May 20

Dear Provo Planning Commission,

I am writing to express my strong opposition to the proposed 20,000-seat Vesper Amphitheater
in Provo Canyon. While restoring the gravel pit is a positive goal, a massive commercial
entertainment venue is entirely inappropriate for this location.

I urge the Commission to deny the requested zone and General Plan changes based on the
following critical impacts:

•       Severe Traffic and Safety Risks: US-189 is already a heavily congested, two-lane
canyon road. Adding up to 20,000 attendees at peak times will create severe traffic gridlock.
This will gridlock regular commuters and dangerously delay emergency vehicles responding to
canyon accidents.

•        Environmental and Wildlife Degradation: The canyon is a vital ecological corridor. The
intense light pollution, late-night noise, and litter generated by massive concerts will disrupt local
wildlife patterns and degrade the natural peace of the canyon. A peace that I enjoy throughout
the year in the canyon.

June 24, 2026                                                                              Page 36

•       Overburdened Infrastructure: A project of this scale will strain local infrastructure,
including parking, trash management, and emergency services, without providing a clear, long-

Provo Canyon should be preserved for low-impact recreation, open space, and natural beauty,
not high-density commercial entertainment. Please protect our canyon and vote against this
proposal.

Sincerely,

Courtney Johnston – May 20

Dear Planning Commission,

While I welcome more opportunities for the arts in our community, the recent announcement
about Vesper Amphitheatre in Provo Canyon has left me more concerned than excited. I live off
of Canyon Road and frequently travel the intersection at Orem's 800 North and Provo's
University Avenue at the mouth of the Canyon. At commute times, this intersection gets
clogged going in all directions. Even more frustrating is the time it takes to get to the I-15 on
ramp on 800 North. The traffic has a domino effect and can cause extremely slow travel that
entire length of road.

Speaking of traffic concerns, there are safety concerns with the proposed number of cars in the
canyon. Left turns across oncoming traffic is especially dangerous at night when visibility is so
low. In order to support the amount of traffic, the infrastructure would have to be improved.
This would likely mean widening University Avenue, 800 North, and even the lanes of the
highway heading into the canyon. This is an expensive and environmentally irresponsible move
to make.

There are environmental concerns as well. Light pollution, sound pollution, and the disturbance
of animal life. The canyon is a refuge for nature lovers and should not be seen as another
frontier to develop.

Tuacahn seats 2,500 people. Red Rocks Amphitheatre seats 9,525. An amphitheatre that
seats 20,000 people is just a ridiculous idea in this location.

Please require the developer to do a deep environmental assessment of their proposed project.
I think you will find that this is not the appropriate location for a venue of this size and scope.

I support protecting the peace and beauty of Provo Canyon and strongly oppose the Vesper
Amphitheatre in its current plans.

Thank you for taking the time to hear my concerns. I know you have a difficult task of balancing
the needs of the private citizens and the city of Provo.

Samuel Bloomfield – May 21

To the Utah County & Provo City Planning Commissions and City Councils,

I am writing to formally state my opposition to the proposed Vesper Amphitheater project and its
associated commercial re-zoning at the mouth of Provo Canyon. I urge the Planning
Commissions and City Councils to vote NO on the commercial re-zoning request and deny any
associated conditional use permits. This massive development fundamentally conflicts with the
preservation mandates outlined in our local General Plans.

I write this as a Utah County resident, a biology major with academic training in local ecology,
and an active researcher in water cycle education. During my early college years, I was entirely
transit-dependent and relied strictly on public transit, accessing the mouth of the canyon on foot.
This canyon gateway served as my only reasonably accessible sensory and psychological
sanctuary from the intense rigors of academic training.
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                             *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                               Page 38

While proponents frame this project as a "harmonious hillside restoration" of a century-old
gravel pit, a rigorous ecological, logistical, and socio-historical analysis reveals severe,
unmitigated flaws in the proposal that violate the spirit of our city and county planning
guidelines:

1. Cumulative Environmental Impact and False Restoration

Provo Canyon has been progressively degraded by heavy industrial and transit projects—from
utility plants to massive highway expansions. Each has historically been approved under the
guise of "mitigation," but the cumulative result is the fragmentation of a vital ecological corridor.
Replacing a mining scar with a high-density, 20,000-seat commercial amphitheater and resort
villas is not ecological restoration; it is permanent urbanization. True restoration should look like
a low-impact conservation easement and re-vegetation, aligning with the critical lands and open
space elements of our General Plans, not a massive commercial venue.

2. Disruption of Hydrological and Riparian Systems

Introducing massive impervious surfaces (roofs, paved gathering spaces, and resort
infrastructure) directly adjacent to the Provo River corridor poses an unmitigated risk to a
delicate riparian buffer zone. It alters local hillside absorption dynamics and introduces urban
runoff directly into a vital watershed at a time when water preservation and watershed protection
are chief priorities explicitly stated in our county’s long-term planning goals.

3. The Myth of the "Harmonious" Hillside Choke Point

Developers have compared this design to open-air desert venues like Tuacahn or Red Rocks.
However, unlike those expansive, isolated landscapes, the mouth of Provo Canyon is a narrow
geographical bottleneck directly adjacent to a major university town. Replicating a stadium
layout here fundamentally traps noise and gridlocks traffic. Furthermore, the proposed enclosed
winter configuration eliminates the seasonal window of absolute silence that local wildlife and
residents rely on for winter seasonal respite.

4. Transit Equity and Sensory Accessibility

The developers’ promise of "free public trail access" ignores the reality of transit-dependent,
low-income, and neurodivergent community members. The mouth of Provo Canyon represents
a vital, low-barrier trailhead that is uniquely accessible to those who cannot easily travel deeper
into the canyon. Developing this specific choke point creates a physical and sensory blockade.

For a pedestrian stepping off the bus to access the nearby trails at Canyon View or Timpanogos
Park, a massive influx of venue crowds effectively destroys the pedestrian-safe buffer zone.
Vulnerable pedestrians cannot peacefully or safely navigate stadium-level crowd densities,
security blockades, and intense sensory pollution on foot just to reach a public trailhead. This
project effectively gentrifies the canyon gateway, walling off nature from the very people who
rely on it for well-being, directly contradicting our community goals for equitable transit and
recreational access.

Conclusion
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                             *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                               Page 39

An objective, systemic look at this project reveals that it simply replaces industrial pollution with
intense sensory, traffic, and environmental pollution. I urge the Commission and City Council to
protect the integrity of our General Plan, reject the commercial re-zoning for this massive venue,
and instead preserve the mouth of Provo Canyon as a quiet, public, and genuinely accessible
sanctuary for the environment and the community alike.

Sincerely,

Samuel B.

Utah County Resident and Ecological Researcher

I found this email address in a comment from Rachel Breen recommending that citizens send

Based on 20,000 seats, there will be a requirement for something like 5,000 parking spots. Each

developer is proposing to develop the 40 acres of city adjacent city owned land for parking.

Mark Allen – May 25

Subject: Formal Request to Suspend Decision Timeline Pending Full Baseline Studies,
Jurisdictional Review, Alternatives Analysis, and Public Impact Disclosure – Proposed Provo
Canyon Development

To: Dspublichearings@provo.gov

Provo Planning Commission
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                          *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                            Page 42

Dear Members of the Planning Commission,

I am submitting this formal public comment regarding the proposed development in Provo
Canyon and requesting that no approvals, entitlements, or construction authorizations proceed
until a complete, transparent, and fully substantiated evidentiary record has been established
and reviewed in the public forum.

Recent indications of near-term groundbreaking raise serious procedural concerns, particularly
given the absence of completed baseline studies in the public record and the lack of a
structured, meaningful public review period proportional to the scale and risk profile of this
proposal.

This matter has also generated significant public attention, including engagement from multiple
community stakeholders, outdoor recreation users, and conservation-minded groups, all of
whom have expressed concerns regarding transportation safety, wildfire risk, canyon
congestion, environmental impacts, and long-term taxpayer burden. These concerns should be
formally addressed within the planning record before any decisions proceed.

1. REQUEST FOR COMPLETE BASELINE STUDIES AND PUBLIC DISCLOSURE

The Planning Commission must require and obtain the full set of baseline studies necessary for
informed decision-making, including:

•      Traffic modeling and peak event impact analysis

•      Emergency evacuation and canyon clearance timing studies

•      Wildland-Urban Interface (WUI) Fire Marshal-reviewed fire behavior analysis

•      Hydrology, stormwater, and watershed impact studies

•      Geotechnical and slope stability evaluations

•      Wildlife corridor and habitat connectivity studies

•      Full fiscal impact analysis, including infrastructure and taxpayer burden exposure

•      Utility, sewer, and water rights and capacity assessments

If these studies have not yet been completed, they must be initiated prior to any further
approvals. The cost of such studies should appropriately be borne by the developer consistent
with standard practice for high-impact developments.

2. FIRE MARSHAL / WILDLAND-URBAN INTERFACE (WUI) SAFETY REQUIREMENTS

Given steep, dry, and heavily vegetated canyon terrain adjacent to wildland fuel sources, a full
Fire Marshal-reviewed WUI analysis is required.

This must include:

•      Evacuation clearance time modeling at peak occupancy
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                           *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                             Page 43

•       Canyon ingress/egress failure and gridlock scenarios

•       Fire apparatus access compliance under applicable fire codes

•       Wildfire behavior modeling (slope-driven spread, canyon wind effects, ember transport)

•       Structure ignition risk and defensible space requirements

•       Emergency evacuation thresholds and shelter-in-place criteria

•       Multi-agency wildfire response coordination planning

In canyon environments, the dominant life-safety risk is evacuation failure under rapidly evolving
wildfire conditions combined with constrained access corridors, not simply structural fire
protection.

3. TRANSPORTATION, HIGHWAY IMPACTS, AND PUBLIC MAINTENANCE BURDEN

US-189 is a federally designated highway corridor eligible for federal-aid funding participation,
with maintenance administered primarily through Utah Department of Transportation.

The proposed development introduces repeated high-intensity traffic loading conditions
including:

•       120+ large-scale annual events

•       8,000–20,000 vehicles per event

•       Severe peak-direction canyon congestion

•       Emergency response interference during surge conditions

•       Increased winter crash risk and roadway stress

This raises a critical fiscal question:

Who bears the long-term cost of accelerated highway wear and event-driven infrastructure
degradation?

While baseline maintenance is publicly funded, repeated peak-load events can significantly
accelerate:

•       Pavement deterioration

•       Winter maintenance burden

•       Accident response and traffic control costs

•       Intersection and signalization upgrades

Unless explicitly conditioned, these costs are effectively socialized onto taxpayers while benefits
are privatized to the venue operator.
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                            *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                              Page 44

A full cumulative transportation impact analysis must include:

•      Event-based pavement wear modeling

•      Lifecycle maintenance cost projections

•      Peak-load structural degradation analysis

•      Full public cost allocation assumptions

4. PUBLIC INFRASTRUCTURE AND TAXPAYER LIABILITY

The record must clearly establish:

•      Road widening and canyon infrastructure expansion funding sources

•      Utility, sewer, and water system upgrade responsibility

•      Emergency services expansion funding obligations

•      Long-term maintenance liabilities for canyon infrastructure

•      Whether any public subsidy is required for project viability

5. FEDERAL LAND ADJACENCY AND NEPA JURISDICTIONAL REVIEW

The project abuts federally managed National Forest land, creating a sensitive interface
between private development and federal land systems.

The Planning Commission must determine whether any federal nexus exists that could trigger
review under the National Environmental Policy Act (NEPA), including:

•      U.S. Forest Service permitting or access requirements

•      U.S. Army Corps of Engineers permits involving waterways

•      Federal Highway Administration involvement through funding or approvals

•      Endangered Species Act consultation requirements

If a federal nexus exists, an Environmental Assessment (EA) or Environmental Impact
Statement (EIS) may be required, including alternatives analysis and cumulative impact review.

No final decision should proceed until this jurisdictional determination is completed and entered
into the public record.

6. PROVO RIVER CLAIMS AND ENFORCEABILITY OF “FISHERY PROTECTION”
STATEMENTS

The applicant has stated that the development will “protect fly fishing” in the Provo River.
However:
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                       *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                         Page 45

•      River ecology and fisheries are managed by state and federal agencies

•      Private developers do not control fish populations or river access outcomes

•      Stormwater compliance does not equate to ecological control

The Planning Commission must require clarification of:

•      Specific legally enforceable mitigation measures

•      Responsible oversight agencies

•      Whether binding agreements exist with Utah Division of Wildlife Resources or related
agencies

•      Measurable environmental performance standards

•      Monitoring and enforcement mechanisms

•      Remedies if degradation occurs despite compliance

Absent enforceable agreements, such claims must be treated as non-binding promotional
statements rather than regulatory commitments.

7. SITE SUITABILITY AND ALTERNATIVES ANALYSIS (HIERARCHY REQUIRED)

A complete alternatives analysis is required to determine whether safer and more appropriate
locations exist.

The following hierarchy of alternatives must be formally evaluated:

Tier 1 – Preferred (Lowest Risk / Highest Infrastructure Capacity)

•      Airport-adjacent development corridors near Provo Airport

o      Existing transportation infrastructure

o      Industrial/commercial zoning compatibility

o      Lower wildfire and slope risk

o      Reduced canyon evacuation constraints

o      Established emergency response access

Tier 2 – Secondary Urban/Infrastructure Corridors

•      Existing commercial and mixed-use zones within Provo/Orem urban footprint

Tier 3 – Transitional Development Areas

•      Utah Lake shoreline and adjacent lower-risk expansion corridors
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                           *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                             Page 46

o      Greater evacuation redundancy

o      Lower wildfire exposure

o      Scalable infrastructure capacity

o      Reduced canyon and slope hazards

Tier 4 – Canyon Corridor (Not Preferred for High-Capacity Assembly Use)

•      Provo Canyon location

o      Severe evacuation constraints

o      Wildfire interface exposure

o      Single-corridor ingress/egress

o      High geotechnical and infrastructure risk

Failure to meaningfully evaluate these alternatives results in an incomplete and procedurally
insufficient record.

8. PROCEDURAL CONCERN: PREMATURE TIMELINE AND INCOMPLETE PUBLIC
PROCESS

Indications of near-term groundbreaking raise serious concern that construction timelines may
be advancing prior to completion of:

•      Baseline technical studies

•      Alternatives analysis

•      Public comment process

•      Jurisdictional determinations

For a project of this magnitude, the public is entitled to a complete evidentiary record before any
irreversible decisions are made.

9. REQUIRED PLANNING COMMISSION ACTION

I respectfully request that the Planning Commission:

•      Suspend any approval or entitlement timeline

•      Require completion and public release of all baseline studies

•      Mandate full alternatives analysis including airport-adjacent and Utah Lake corridors

•      Require Fire Marshal-reviewed WUI evacuation modeling

•      Require full fiscal impact disclosure including transportation lifecycle costs
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                           *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                             Page 47

•      Complete NEPA jurisdictional determination where applicable

•      Ensure meaningful public comment after full disclosure

•      Make formal site suitability findings only after full record completion

CLOSING STATEMENT

This submission is not a request for delay without justification. It is a request that decisions be
made on a complete, transparent, and verifiable evidentiary record consistent with public safety,
fiscal responsibility, environmental stewardship, and lawful planning procedure.

A high-capacity development in a constrained canyon wildfire corridor cannot be responsibly
evaluated without full baseline studies, jurisdictional clarity, and meaningful alternatives
analysis.

For these reasons, I respectfully request that the Planning Commission obtain all necessary
data, complete the public record, and defer any decision until a fully informed evaluation is
possible. Multiple public hearings should be held over the next two years while baseline data is
gathered for a project of this scope with significant negative impact on taxpayers and the
canyon corridor.

Thank you for your time and consideration.

Respectfully,

Mark Allen

Founder, Protect and Preserve American Fork Canyon



Matt Peterson – May 26

It was with shock and great alarm that I read the headlines in several papers last weekend,
announcing the planned construction of a 20,000-seat amphitheater across from Canyon View
and Timpanogos Parks in the mouth of Provo Canyon!

A snarling congested "Los Angeles-type” gridlock immediately came to mind (caused by the
thousands of cars needed to transport 20,000 theater goers, many, if not most of whom would
be converging on the parking lots just minutes before showtime).

Then my mind shot to the size of parking lot that would have to be provided for that size of a
theater!

Assuming in the best possible circumstances that people would be thoughtful enough to car-
pool, the likely maximum number of passengers per vehicle would be 4. (More likely, the
number of passengers would be 2)! Either way, the math boils down to this: to transport 20,000
people in private vehicles would require from 5,000-10,000 separate vehicles (which would
require a corresponding number of parking spaces).
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                               *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                                 Page 48

Out of curiosity, I asked AI to tell me more about the number of parking spots at SLC airport (to
get a visual idea for how big the parking lot would have to be to accommodate that many
vehicles). What I learned astounded me! (See first attached screenshot)

According to AI, in order to provide parking for approximately 6,000 vehicles, SLC airport has
dedicated 1.8 million square feet of uncovered + covered parking (in the terrace). This could be
calculated/confirmed as 1.8 million sq ft (calculated by multiplying 300 sq ft….the typical space
required for one parking place and associated driving lanes…. times 6,000 vehicles = 300 x
6,000 = 1.8 million), which is equivalent to 31.3 football fields!!!

The number of football fields needed to park 7,000 vehicles would be (7,000 vehicles x 300 sq
ft per vehicle divided by 57,600 sq ft per football field = 36 football fields). So, unless the plan is
to bus the patrons for the megatheater in… what will be the environmental impact of driving and
parking between 5,000-10,000 vehicles in the mouth of Provo canyon?

Out of curiosity I searched the internet to see how traffic created by the Osmond Megatheater
would compare to traffic in Big Cottonwood Canyon during ski season. According to info shown
in the second attached screenshot, peak traffic in Big Cottonwood maxes out (ideally) at 1,000
cars per hour!!

At the rate of 1,000 cars per hour, it would take up to at least 5 hours to get 5,000 cars carrying
4 passengers each in order to fill the 20,000 foot theater!! Then, given that it is more likely that
people will travel in pairs of 2, it is much more likely to require up to 10,000 vehicles to transport
20,000 passengers (2 per vehicle) (which would require 10 hours at 1,000 cars per hour…the
Big Cottonwood Canyon maximum rate on peak season snow days!!)

Dialing those figures down a bit, if we assume 3 passengers per vehicle, it would require
between 6,000-7,000 vehicles to transport 20,000 people (3 per car), and require 6-7 hours at
the maximum Big Cottonwood Canyon rate of 1,000 cars per hour!!!

The environmental impact of this many vehicles on the road at the mouth of Provo must be
astronomical, not to mention the impact of covering 36 football fields of canyon land with asphalt
or concrete for parking!

Of equal concern to me is the possibility that in order to accommodate that many people, while
reducing the need for on-site parking, a parking hub somewhere in Orem or Provo would need
to be built (similar to the one proposed for Big Cottonwood Canyon).

As you can see in the attached screenshot that shows info regarding the proposed parking hub
for Big Cottonwood Canyon (BCC), they are suggesting that busses will depart every 7-15
minutes in order to deliver the passengers of 1750 vehicles to ski resorts further on up the
canyon. But if you do the math for the proposed Osmond Megatheater, even if all 1750
vehicles had only 2 passengers, it would take a typical BCC-size bus (total capacity 60 including
standees), 58 trips to transport 1750x2 passengers (3500 passengers). And that’s only about
1/6 as many patrons as the proposed 20,000 megatheater seats!!!

If you think about even those numbers for a few minutes: approx 60 bus trips would be needed
to transport 3600 people from a central Orem or Provo based parking hub, either one trip per
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                              *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                                Page 49

each of the 60 buses, or 10 trips for each of 6 buses. If we go with the latter figure (6 buses
making 10 trips), all 6 buses would have to do each single trip in 6 minutes (so each bus would
be able to make 10 trips per hour). If we change the math, and use 10 buses, each bus would
need to make the trip 6 times ( 6 trips per hour x 10 buses x 60 passengers per busload = 3600
passengers delivered per hour).

At that rate, it would take 10 buses a total of approximately 5 hours to transport all 20,000
people to the megatheater.

These numbers may not take into consideration algorithms that UDOT has for taking into
account private vehicle traffic, but even if they are only approximate, they illustrate the
enormous problem associated with moving 20,000 people up that canyon…perhaps on a
biweekly basis!!!

According to info on the internet, the rate of traffic on I-15 during peak rush hour traffic is 1500-
1900 vehicles per hour per lane. If we assume each private vehicle transporting people to the
Megatheater carries 3 passengers, we can calculate the amount of time needed to transport
20,000 people via private vehicles using the same rate ( 20,000/3 x 1/1900 x 1/2 lanes = 1.8
hours)(this assumes a maximum rate of 3800 vehicles per hour, on the existing 2 lanes of
highway and 3 passengers per vehicle). If we assume only 1500 per hour per lane (x 2 lanes),
the total time changes to 2.2 hours. And the numbers increase if we assume the more realistic
occupancy of 2 passengers per vehicle to 2.7 to 3.3 hours!!! (And at least 36 football fields of
either concrete or asphalt to park them…36 if we assume only approx. 7000 vehicles, but more
if the plan is to provide parking for 10,000 )!!!

For all of the above reasons (and more), I strongly feel that building this megatheater is NOT in
Utah Valley’s best interest! It is bad for Provo, bad for Orem, and bad for Utah Valley! (Not to
mention the Canyon…which according to some world-views has a Spirit that should be
respected, cared for, protected, and nurtured)!

Please, please, please….for the sake of Utah Valley residents and the beautiful Canyon itself,
do not allow this mismanagement of one of our most beautiful natural resources to go
unchallenged!! We will be reaping the very negative rewards of a very near-sighted decision for
decades to come if we proceed with building this Megatheater!

Matt A. Peterson

Concerned Orem City Resident

Hello commission members - I'm sure you've heard about the Vesper project in the mouth of

range, fly fishing, recreating, trails, affordability and tax increases for local residents, etc.

and is, "just because you can, doesn't mean you should." You know we are not against
development. We are happy to finally have a great plan with Reza Fakhrieh for new housing in
the NE foothills. We have been so happy to have a mayor and council members who treasure

to pressure to see this through. We will lose the unique treasure that Provo Canyon is - that we
have fought to maintain.

Senator Keven Stratton told us when we were working to get Bridal Veil Falls into state
monument status that he would be protective about the whole Provo Canyon corridor. It's that
kind of thinking and determination that will leave a beautiful, natural canyon for many of our
progeny for generations to come.

You are the gatekeepers of this decision. Please, please choose wisely!

Kaye Nelson

you are now met with the view of massive development - amphitheater, hotels, restaurants,
parking lots, a bridge, heavy traffic that is dangerous and unmanageable, noise and light
pollution. The gateway to the natural sanctuary of Provo Canyon is gone forever. Further up the
canyon, additional development is in works – due to the flood gates being opened from this
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                              *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                                Page 51

project. That is the reality of Provo Canyon if the Vesper Amphitheater development project is
allowed to move forward.

Just as the owners of Vesper (Spencer Schumway) and David Osmond sought to expand
Splash Summit Water Park in Provo, to the base of Slate Canyon (which was shut down, largely

that will encroach on a different canyon – this time Provo Canyon. Regardless of their spin or
who they put in front of a microphone, this is not primarily about the arts – this is simply about
development and profit. Same cast of characters – just a different location, a different narrative
they are pushing and a different canyon that would be ruined.

The recent announcement of this project has nothing to do with concepts like ‘preserve’,
‘protect’, ‘restoration’, ‘conservation’, ‘stewardship’, being ‘caretakers’, etc. The fact that words
like this are mentioned in the same breath of announcing this project is not only disingenuous,

seconds, as their gaze is drawn forward to Mount Timpanogos, the canyon walls and the trees
in front of them. The quarry does not bring in thousands of additional cars/ traffic, noise and light
pollution, and development. The ‘eyesore’ of a gravel pit barely noticed is nothing compared to
the damage this development project will cause.

For many of us, Provo Canyon represents a natural sanctuary and a sacred space. I grew up
near the mouth of Provo Canyon and have spent countless hours up this precious canyon
personally, with my wife and with my family – hiking, skiing, fishing, working at Bridal Veil Falls,
mountain biking, picnicking, taking walks, and just basking in the beauty of it’s canyon walls.
Places like Squaw (Khyv) Peak, Canyon Glenn, Bridal Veil, Nunns Park, Sundance, Alpine
Loop, etc. will be heavily impacted by this development project. If it is allowed to go through,
the peace and serenity of this canyon sanctuary will simply disappear all too quickly.

There are plenty of music venues in Utah – but there is only one Provo Canyon. There really is
no place like it in the world. Because of that, let us preserve this canyon - not exploit it for the
sake of economic gain for a handful of those that will benefit financially. The arts are extremely
important and great venues contribute to an amazing experience – but Provo Canyon is
absolutely not the place for a massive development project like this.

I urge you to please do all you can to oppose this project and help ensure it gets shut down.
Help us preserve this amazing canyon – for us and for future generations.

Thank you very much!

John Moss

Brandon Barnes – May 29

My name is Brandon Barnes, and I would like to express my opposition to the proposed zone
map amendment (Item #5 and #6) for the entertainment and hospitality development in Provo
Canyon.

I want to be clear that this is not simply a proposal to redevelop an existing gravel pit. The
narrative being presented suggests that a disturbed industrial site will be transformed into
something more productive. However, the application before you goes far beyond the footprint
of the existing gravel site, which is a mere 24 acres.

The developers are requesting that approximately 100 additional acres be rezoned to support
this project. Those acres are not a gravel pit. They consist of foothills, open space, and
recreational land currently enjoyed by residents and visitors. This proposal would convert land
that belongs to the public realm into land serving a private commercial enterprise.

That distinction matters.

If a private landowner wishes to develop land already zoned and designated for that purpose,
that is one discussion. What is being requested here is different. The public is being asked to
surrender open space, recreational value, and the character of the canyon in order to facilitate a
private profit-making venture.

I am also deeply concerned about transportation and public safety.

For comparison, Utah First Credit Union Amphitheatre occupies roughly 90 acres and is served
by major multi-lane transportation corridors. Even with that infrastructure, traffic congestion
before and after events is substantial.

This proposal contemplates a venue of similar scale at the entrance to a canyon served by a
single four-lane highway that functions as a regional recreation corridor. Thousands of
additional vehicles attempting to enter and exit a 20,000-seat venue through this bottleneck
would create predictable congestion, safety concerns, emergency access challenges, and
significant impacts on nearby communities and canyon users.

The question before the Council is not whether concerts are enjoyable or whether economic
development is valuable. The question is whether this particular location is appropriate for a
project of this scale.
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                           *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                             Page 54

Provo Canyon is one of the most iconic natural and recreational resources in Utah County.
Once open space is rezoned and developed, it is effectively gone forever. Future generations
will not have the opportunity to reclaim it.

I urge the Council to look beyond the gravel pit narrative and evaluate the full scope of what is
being requested: the conversion of over 100 acres of open and recreational land, the
introduction of a massive regional entertainment venue into a canyon environment, and the
long-term impacts that decision will have on residents, recreation, transportation, and the
character of the area.

Not every valuable piece of land must be converted into a commercial venture. Directly across
the highway, Orem City has demonstrated another approach by preserving a similar property for
public recreation and trail access. Decades from now, I believe residents will be far more
grateful for protected open space than for additional parking lots and traffic congestion.

For those reasons, I respectfully ask you to deny the requested zone amendment. Thank you.

Talon Patten – June 1

Dear Members of the City Council and Planning Commission,
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                             *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                               Page 64

I am writing to express my concerns regarding the proposed Vesper Amphitheater development
at the mouth of Provo Canyon.

While I understand the desire to redevelop the existing gravel pit site, I believe this project
raises serious questions about traffic, noise, environmental impacts, watershed protection,
commercialization of the canyon, and the long term character of one of Utah County’s most
important recreational areas.

My first concern is traffic. I work at BYU and regularly experience the effects of large events on
local transportation. Even events with attendance around 5,000 people can create severe
congestion for hours before and after an event. The difference is that BYU and central Provo
have an extensive network of roads and alternate routes that allow traffic to disperse in multiple
directions.

Provo Canyon does not have that advantage.

The canyon effectively operates on a single road corridor. A venue designed for 20,000
attendees could potentially generate thousands of vehicles arriving and departing within a
relatively short period of time. If traffic becomes gridlocked, there are very few alternative routes
available. I am concerned that residents, emergency services, recreation users, and visitors
could all be negatively impacted by recurring congestion during concert events.

As for the residents that live in the canyon, and the residents that live on both sides of the
canyon like Orem and Provo and Heber, I dont see how they will be able to make it home during
these hours of activity.

My second concern is noise.

I currently live in Springdell, and despite being nearly a mile from the gun range, it is common to
hear gunshots echoing through the canyon, particularly during quiet evening hours. If a few
shotgun blasts can be heard at that distance, it is reasonable to ask how far amplified concert
sound carrying tens of thousands of watts of audio equipment will travel.

Provo Canyon is valued because it provides an escape from the city. Many people visit the
canyon for hiking, biking, camping, fishing, wildlife viewing, and simply enjoying a natural
environment. Introducing large-scale concert noise into the canyon environment seems
fundamentally incompatible with those uses.

The issue of noise in recreational landscapes is not a new one. National parks such as the
Grand Canyon have spent decades addressing concerns over noise pollution because visitors
value natural quiet. I question whether Utah County should knowingly create a similar conflict in
one of its most heavily used recreation corridors.

I am also concerned about the watershed implications of the broader development plan.

The project materials discuss hotels, cafes, restaurants, retail spaces, and other commercial
development. Given the importance of Provo Canyon as a watershed area, I believe the public
deserves detailed answers regarding water quality protection, runoff management, stormwater
infrastructure, and long-term environmental impacts.
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                            *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                              Page 65

Parking is another major concern.

A 20,000-seat venue requires a tremendous amount of parking. Even conservative estimates
suggest thousands of vehicles would need to be accommodated. That means large areas of
pavement, parking lots, access roads, lighting, and associated infrastructure. I worry that what is
currently a natural canyon entrance will gradually be transformed into a sea of asphalt.

Beyond the immediate project, I am concerned about the precedent this development could
establish.

If one developer is allowed to build hotels, restaurants, retail spaces, and entertainment venues
in the canyon, what prevents future developments from requesting similar treatment? What
begins as one project could gradually lead to further commercialization of the canyon.

Many residents have watched places such as Park City become increasingly dominated by
tourism oriented development, rising costs, and private interests. I worry that Provo Canyon
could begin moving in the same direction. This canyon should remain a place that is accessible
and enjoyable for everyone, not just those who can afford expensive lodging, entertainment, or
commercial recreation.

I am also troubled by the accuracy of the project’s public renderings. Some renderings appear
to misrepresent the size, scale, or alignment of existing roads and surrounding features. If the
public is being asked to evaluate a project of this magnitude, the materials presented should
accurately reflect existing conditions. If basic elements are not represented correctly, it raises
concerns about what other impacts may be underestimated or overlooked.

I believe there are better alternatives for this site.

If redevelopment is desired, I would encourage consideration of a more limited project focused
on public recreation and open space rather than large-scale commercial development. For
example:

• Eliminate the proposed hotels, retail spaces, and shopping areas.

• Preserve as much open space as possible.

• Create public parks similar to Timpanogos Park.

• Develop trail connections and recreational amenities.

• Consider mountain biking facilities, outdoor recreation infrastructure, or other low-impact uses.

• If an amphitheater is pursued, significantly reduce its scale and separate it from extensive
commercial development.

Provo Canyon is one of Utah County’s greatest natural assets. Decisions made today will shape
the canyon for generations. I respectfully ask that city leaders carefully consider whether a
20,000-seat entertainment complex, combined with hotels, retail development, and extensive
parking infrastructure, is truly consistent with the character, environmental value, and
recreational purpose of the canyon.
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                             *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                               Page 66

Most importantly, once a natural resource like this is gone, it is gone forever. Think of all the
natural wonders of the world that have disappeared, never to come back again, due to the
greed of humans. Don't be on the wrong side of history on this subject.

Thank you for your time and consideration.

Sincerely,

FREDERICK TALON PATTEN

Dan Moss – June 2

To the Provo City Mayor, City Council, and Utah County Commissioner,

I am writing to express my strong opposition to the proposed Vesper Amphitheatre development
in Provo Canyon.

I understand that the site has previously been used as a gravel pit, and I support thoughtful
efforts to reclaim and improve disturbed land. However, a large entertainment venue that could
bring thousands of people, vehicles, lights, and noise into the canyon is not restoration. It is a
major development project in one of the most sensitive and beloved natural areas in our
community.

Provo Canyon is already a constrained transportation corridor. Adding a venue of this size
raises serious concerns about traffic congestion, public safety, emergency access, parking,
evacuation, and the potential need for expensive road and infrastructure improvements. Before
any approval is given, the city should require a full traffic impact analysis, including event-night
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                           *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                             Page 68

congestion, winter conditions, emergency response, downstream bottlenecks, and who would
pay for any required improvements.

I am also deeply concerned about the environmental impacts. Provo Canyon is an important
recreation area, wildlife corridor, watershed, and natural refuge. A development of this scale
could affect wildlife movement, increase vehicle-wildlife collisions, harm dark-sky conditions,
create noise that carries through the canyon, and increase runoff, erosion, and pollution risks
near the Provo River watershed. These impacts should not be minimized or rushed through.

The public deserves a meaningful voice in any decision that would permanently change Provo
Canyon. This canyon belongs to the broader community, and decisions about its future should
prioritize long-term public safety, environmental protection, recreation access, and responsible
stewardship over private entertainment development.

I respectfully ask Provo City to oppose this proposal unless and until these serious concerns are
fully studied, publicly reviewed, and satisfactorily addressed. In my view, this land would be
better suited for lower-impact reclamation, habitat restoration, open-use recreation, or wildlife
connectivity improvements rather than a large amphitheatre that could create permanent and
recurring impacts on the canyon.

Thank you for your time and service to our community.

Sincerely,

Dan Moss



Elliott Nelson – June 2

Greetings,

Thank you for your service to our community.

The meeting last evening with the City Council and Bryan Bayles was very informative. I
appreciate the process the city goes through to listen to all sides. I came away with some
helpful new insights and changed my thinking on some issues. The value of such a forum is that
so many different aspects of a project like this are brought to the surface and we get to hear
many different views of how people value this land – more on that later.

For me, two things stood out from Mr. Bayles’ presentation:

1.     The size of this project – a 20,000 seat amphitheater was pitched as the only way to
make this a viable project and repurpose the gravel pit. Despite many raising the examples of
Red Rocks, Tuacahn and other venues with great outdoor experience (more intimate), the
developers say that Vesper only works if it has a 20K capacity. And certain artists will only play
a venue that size. More thought needs to go into what kind of experience we want and whether
there might be different options for size. The ‘size urgency’ seems pressured. And if their
finances are dependent on that condition, the project seems less tenable.
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                          *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                            Page 69

2.      The project is not realistic in how it addresses additional traffic flow and safety on Hwy
189 or parking for events of different sizes. This highway is already crowded and dangerous and
the venue sits between 2 blind curves. And it is a major throughway to Heber, Park City and
beyond. Bayles went from saying they would have mostly small events to saying they would
pack it full as often as possible. And where to put parking and the ingress and egress for 8,000
spaces was never fully addressed.

I came to the meeting hoping to hear how problems are being addressed to gain confidence in
the development team. I wanted to give this project a chance. I felt Bayles pandered somewhat
to the audience – by bringing up the kind of care they would take to the site and general
categories of problems – but then saying essentially that we need to ‘trust them’ to do the right
thing.

 The site images shared seemed unrealistic. We saw a site map overlay on the gravel pit and an
artists’ conception that both showed around 500 parking spaces whereas according to the plan,
there will be at least 8,000 spaces. There was talk of a parking structure partially underground,
but this raises questions about cost and feasibility given the rock and groundwater issues.

Here is where I feel we need more details on how it would look & operate and perhaps some
working models of the site:

Parking for 8,000 spaces takes up 55-65 acres. Parking garages of this size are found at major
US airports (link) and theme parks like Disney and Universal. DFW has a garage holding 8,100
cars that is 8 levels spread over approx. 9 acres. What is the size of the parking structure this
site needs at a minimum to accommodate 8,000 cars? The 5-level parking garage at SLC holds
3,600 cars and is 900 feet long.

 The key difference between structures of a similar size and the proposed parking structure for
Vesper is that these airport facilities fill and empty one car at a time over a day – a trickle of
cars. And they make money 24-7. Whereas Vesper guests would want to enter or leave as
quickly as possible, and the structure would only be able to charge when a concert came along.
Imagine how long it would take SLC or DFW garages to empty if everyone were asked to leave
at the same time! What level of traffic control manning this garage would be required to expedite
filling and emptying for large events?

How would a structure of that size fit on this property and how would the financials work if it
were empty most of the time? Would we want a garage of this size at this site in Provo Canyon?

 Does it make sense to have that much parking for the limited number of events that would
require it? This gets back to the ‘size urgency’. There are several important financial and
environmental questions around this issue to be resolved before the project is viable.

 I’m an ardent conservationist. I want to see Provo Canyon preserved. But I am also pragmatic
and if this project is what the city wants to do, these issues would have to be resolved before
any change in zoning is granted. I feel the developers are pushing this as a big sales job and
asking us to take their expertise on ‘faith’.
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                              *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                                Page 70

There is one unifying reason why so many people came last night opposed to this project: this
canyon, like all our mountain environs – is why we live here and indeed what makes life
possible in this valley. It is the source of our water supply, it provides a close-by refuge of cool
and shade in the summer. We can get to skiing in 20 minutes in the winter.

 Our ‘crown jewels’ are already there and the hand of man cannot ‘restore’ it or make it better
than what it is. Once we start developing inside the canyon at scale, we cannot reclaim that
treasure. Maybe Provo and this valley do need a venue of this size and the revenue it brings,
but why does it need to be inside Provo Canyon, especially given these complex, unanswered
questions?

Thank you for your consideration of these thoughts & questions.

Best regards,

Elliott

Provo

Brandon Barnes – June 2

I am writing to urge you to oppose the proposed rezoning associated with the planned
amphitheater development in Provo Canyon. Before looking at site plans, traffic studies, or
economic projections, I would ask you to do something much simpler:

Drive up Squaw Peak Road (now Kyhv) and stop near the first crest where the view opens to
the west. Or hike the Bonneville Shoreline Trail above the site. Take a moment to look across
those foothills and open space. Then imagine them gone, or at best heavily distorted. Not the
24-acre gravel pit. Most people can agree that disturbed industrial land should be improved and
put to better use. Imagine everything surrounding it.

Imagine roads cut into the hillsides. Imagine acres of parking. Imagine lighting, fencing, utility
infrastructure, traffic controls, and all of the other elements required to support a venue
designed for 20,000 people, along with the proposed hospitality and commercial development.
Because that is the reality of what a project of this scale requires.

The gravel pit itself is only 24 acres. Yet the proposal seeks to rezone approximately 100 acres
of land. More than 75 percent of the land involved in this project is not the gravel pit. It is open
space, foothills, and recreational land. That distinction matters. The public discussion has
focused heavily on the redevelopment of a gravel pit, but a 20,000 seat amphitheater, hotel
villas, restaurants and shopping are not a 24 acre project. They cannot be.

For comparison, UFCU Amphitheatre (formerly USANA) in West Valley accommodates
approximately 25,000 guests. The venue occupies a massive developed site and is surrounded
by extensive parking. Roughly 50 acres of that property are dedicated to parking alone. Even
with major transportation infrastructure, including multiple arterial roadways and direct regional
access, traffic entering and leaving events is typically frustrating and time-consuming.

Now imagine attempting to accommodate nearly the same number of guests at the entrance to
Provo Canyon. Not in the middle of an urban commercial district. Not adjacent to multiple four
and six-lane roadways. At a canyon bottleneck served primarily by a highway with only two
lanes in each direction. The transportation challenge seems obvious. More importantly, the
physical footprint required to support that number of attendees has to go somewhere.
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                              *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                                Page 73

Another comparison worth considering is Tuacahn in St. George. Tuacahn sits on just about 20
acres, including its amphitheater and parking areas, and accommodates roughly 2,500 guests.
It is a destination venue that fits the scale and character of its surroundings. This proposal
seeks to place a venue roughly eight times larger in one of the most scenic and heavily used
recreational gateways in Utah County.

I am not opposed to improving the gravel pit parcel. If private owners wish to purchase and
develop that land, there are many possibilities that could fit within its footprint while respecting
the character of the canyon. What I struggle to understand is why a 24 acre gravel pit requires
100 acres of rezoning and development.

Across the highway, Orem City has demonstrated a different vision. Similar canyon-adjacent
land has been preserved for recreation, trail access, and public enjoyment. Those decisions
continue to benefit residents every day.

As a Planning Commision member, you are not simply deciding whether an amphitheater
should be built. You are deciding whether more than 100 acres of open space, foothills, and
recreational land should be converted to support a private commercial venture. Once these
foothills are developed, they will never return to what they are today.

I respectfully ask that you look beyond the renderings and marketing materials and focus on the
long-term consequences of this rezoning request.

Please vote no.

Thank you for your time and consideration.

Sincerely,

A Very Concerned Resident

Brandon Barnes

Jack Donaldson – June 4

Dear Commissioner(s),

I am writing in strong opposition to the proposed Vesper Amphitheater development at the
mouth of Provo Canyon. I'm a longtime Orem resident and a regular user of the canyon — for
hiking, biking, and the kind of quiet, restorative experience that Provo Canyon uniquely provides
along the Wasatch Front.

I understand the appeal of ambitious development, and I don't doubt that some people are
excited about this project. But I believe the vast majority of residents and canyon users would
be negatively affected by it — and I'd ask that you weigh their interests heavily in your
deliberations.

The scale is simply incompatible with this location. A 20,000-seat outdoor venue is roughly
twelve times larger than Tuacahn and twice the size of Red Rocks. For comparison, the Marriott
Center at BYU — which holds events of similar size — requires an enormous dedicated parking
infrastructure surrounded by an entire university campus. The mouth of Provo Canyon has none
of that. A single canyon road serving 20,000 concertgoers isn't a traffic inconvenience — it's a
public safety crisis. Emergency access, evacuation routes, and the basic flow of canyon traffic
would all be severely compromised.

The environmental harm would be permanent. This is the opposite of a “restoration.” Light
pollution from a venue of this scale would fundamentally alter the character of a canyon that
has, until now, remained largely undeveloped. Noise pollution and water aquifer risks compound
those concerns. Wildlife would be displaced. These aren't speculative worries — they are the
predictable consequences of Commercializing a narrow canyon environment.

I'll note a small irony that isn't lost on me: across the highway from Timpanogos View Park sits a
gravel pit — not ideal, but at least contained. The Vesper Amphitheater would be many times
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                            *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                              Page 80

more disruptive and far more permanent. Whatever one thinks of the gravel pit, at least it
doesn't draw 20,000 people on a Friday night.

Provo Canyon belongs to all of us. It is one of the most accessible and beloved natural spaces
along the entire Wasatch Front. I strongly urge you to reject this proposal and protect the
canyon from a development that serves the few at the expense of the many.

Thank you for your time and public service.

Sincerely,

Jack Donaldson, PhD

Long-time Orem Resident

Regular User of Provo Canyon

Hello Provo Planning Commission,

I am a local avid trail runner, mountain biker, and enjoyer of all parts of Provo Canyon. In light of
the recent announcement of the Vesper Amphitheater, I have several major concerns about the
feasibility and reality of this project. Here are some of the main questions that I would like to see
thoroughly answered and given to the public before any further action is taken on this project.

- The Zone Change, Concept Plan, and General Plan Map Amendment says you ask that Provo
City rezone 100.7 acres, but Steve Turley only owns 24 acres at his gravel pit. Where will the
other acres come from?

- Additionally, using Google Earth and the current renderings, the entire project will take up
about 64 acres, yet the project is claiming to have "50+ conservation easement acres".

- What easements will you need from the Forest Service? Have you begun the process with
them?

- Where are those acres located, and how will the transition of property ownership happen?

Alongside this, currently, the shooting range affects where people like me can hike, run, and
bike. Will the city need to move the shooting range?

- Construction is planned on an area with steep slopes and an underground parking garage that
may compromise slope stability (rockfall, landslides, slope creep, freeze -thaw instability,
earthquake amplification, avalanche). What engineering firm has been contacted or hired, and
what have they told you about this project? I encourage the city to require a full geotechnical
study before the planning commission or the city council considers rezoning.

- A 20,000-person venue could create multi-hour exit times, gridlock extending miles
downstream, air pollution inversion effects, and emergency response delays. The public may
ultimately subsidize hundreds of millions in road widening and traffic mitigation for a private
entertainment development.

- Additionally, will the UTA ski bus service to Sundance be affected by traffic in the winter due to
the year-round nature of at least 8,000 seats in the amphitheater?

- What taxpayer-funded improvements are anticipated for the area?

- What are the long-term maintenance costs for necessary highway improvements in Provo
Canyon?

- Who will pay for snow removal during events? Will this be managed by the city?
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                              *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                                Page 82

- What studies have been requested from UDOT for traffic issues in the canyon? I will
encourage the city to require such studies, as the canyon is already very crowded at peak
times, and this will likely only increase congestion.

- The architectural drawing and renderings are preliminary and thus, not detailed. What plans
has your architect made for emergency staging areas, ride-share queues, bus
loading/unloading, ADA overflow, and fire evacuation? Additionally, how can the public trust the
renderings generated by AI to be accurate in terms of how this project will turn out?

Foremost, I strongly and highly encourage the city to require a complete environmental impact
study before the planning commission and the city council consider rezoning. I hope that any
such study will include consideration of how the resort will affect wildlife migration, breeding,
and genetic connectivity. Has the DWR modeled these displacement effects? How much will
wildlife vehicle collisions increase? What effect will the increased impervious surfaces have on
water flow? How will this affect the wildlife of the area with noise pollution?

- What is the estimate for the peak decibels during concerts? How much will this affect migratory
bird orientation?

- What is your estimate for peak and average light pollution? How will the loss of dark sky
conditions disrupt nocturnal wildlife behavior?

- How much do you estimate air pollution will increase from vehicles and buildings (heating and
cooling), not just the 120 days for concert traffic, but the daily traffic of visitors to the hotels,
restaurants, and shops?

Because this development will profoundly affect the identity of both Provo and the canyon, I ask
what feasibility studies you’ve completed, as Provo already has concert venues at BYU, Covey
Center for the Arts, SCERA, the Ruth, and Sundance summer theaters. What economic
analysis have you conducted to measure the need for another concert venue and resort?

Vesper's promotional materials state that "94%" of Provo residents support the plan. How did
they calculate that number? What public surveys has the city conducted, or will it conduct?

What other areas have been considered for this project?

John Bingham – June 8

Dear Provo City Planning Commission:

I am writing regarding the proposed Vesper Amphitheater applications: PLGPA20260220,
PLRZ20260221, and PLCP20260222. I appreciate the opportunity to comment, and I recognize
that the existing gravel-pit condition at the mouth of Provo Canyon is not ideal. I am not
opposed to responsible reclamation of disturbed land, improved public access, or thoughtful trail
connections. However, I do not support the proposed General Plan amendment, rezoning, and
concept plan in their current form.

My concern is not simply with the idea of an amphitheater. My concern is with the scale,
location, zoning implications, and sequencing of the public decision. A 20,000-seat outdoor
venue, an 8,000-seat indoor/winter configuration, hotel facilities, restaurants, shops, parking
structures, and related commercial uses would represent a major intensification of use at one of
the most sensitive and constrained locations in Provo: the mouth of Provo Canyon. This is not a
routine redevelopment parcel. It is a gateway to a canyon that serves as a transportation
corridor, recreation corridor, wildlife area, river corridor, and defining natural feature for Provo
and Utah Valley.

For that reason, I respectfully urge the Planning Commission and City Council not to approve
the requested rezoning and General Plan amendment at this stage.

The central issue is sequencing. The city and the public should not be asked to approve broad
commercial zoning before the full impacts of the project are independently studied, publicly
disclosed, and meaningfully evaluated. Once land is rezoned to SC3, the public has already
conferred substantial land-use value and political momentum on the project. It would be difficult,
as a practical matter, to return to a neutral decision posture after that entitlement is granted. The
studies should precede the entitlement, not follow it.

Before any rezoning or General Plan amendment is approved, I believe the city should require
and publish independent analyses of at least the following issues:

Traffic and evacuation. The proposed venue could place thousands of vehicles into a narrow
canyon corridor before and after events. The city should require a rigorous traffic analysis,
coordinated with UDOT and neighboring jurisdictions, that evaluates ingress, egress,
downstream intersection failure, emergency access, evacuation time, pedestrian and bicycle
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                             *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                               Page 86

conflicts, transit feasibility, rideshare staging, winter conditions, and event overlap with canyon
recreation traffic.

Public safety and emergency response. A high-capacity venue at the canyon mouth creates
serious questions about fire response, ambulance access, police staffing, crowd movement, and
emergency evacuation. The city should not rely on general assurances. It should require a
detailed public-safety plan and an estimate of recurring public costs.

Geotechnical and hydrologic risk. The site includes steep slopes and a history of disturbance.
Before rezoning, the city should require a geotechnical and hydrologic analysis addressing
slope stability, rockfall, landslide risk, stormwater, groundwater, freeze-thaw conditions, seismic
considerations, underground parking feasibility, and effects on the Provo River corridor.

Wildlife and environmental impact. The canyon is not merely a scenic backdrop. It is part of a
larger ecological system. The city should require formal wildlife and habitat analysis, including
review by appropriate state wildlife officials, before approving a commercial zone change. The
analysis should address migration, breeding, nocturnal behavior, light pollution, sound
propagation, vehicle-wildlife collisions, and cumulative impacts.

Noise and lighting. Sound and light behave differently in canyon environments than in ordinary
urban sites. The city should require enforceable standards for decibel levels, hours of operation,
sound directionality, lighting design, dark-sky compliance, monitoring, penalties, and event
curfews before any approval is granted.

Water, wastewater, utilities, and infrastructure costs. The public should know what infrastructure
is required, who will pay for it, who will maintain it, and whether Provo taxpayers will assume
direct or indirect costs for a private entertainment and hospitality development.

Public land and long-term land control. The proposal appears to involve more than the privately
owned gravel-pit parcel. If city-owned land, public-facility land, open-space land, shooting-range
land, easements, or land exchanges are involved, those details should be disclosed before any
land-use approval. The public should know exactly which parcels are affected, who owns them,
what will be sold or exchanged, what restrictions will be attached, and what happens if the
amphitheater is not built.

I am also concerned about the breadth of the proposed SC3 zoning. If the stated public benefit
is reclamation, conservation, and a specific amphitheater concept, then the city should not grant
broad commercial zoning that could later support materially different development. Any
approval, if considered at all, should be narrowly conditioned on the specific project represented
to the public. It should not become a general commercial entitlement at the mouth of Provo
Canyon.

If the city proceeds despite these concerns, I respectfully ask that any approval include binding,
enforceable conditions, including:

1.     Reversion of zoning if the amphitheater project proves infeasible, is abandoned, is
materially altered, or is not constructed within a defined time period.
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                           *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                             Page 87

2.     Prohibition on unrelated commercial development if the amphitheater does not proceed.

3.   Preservation of existing OSPR land wherever possible, rather than rezoning land to
commercial status only to later place some portion of it in a conservation easement.

4.      Binding limits on maximum attendance, event frequency, event hours, sound levels,
lighting levels, parking capacity, and commercial uses.

5.      Public release and review of all traffic, environmental, geotechnical, hydrologic, sound,
lighting, public-safety, utility, and fiscal-impact studies before final approval.

6.     Clear allocation of all infrastructure, policing, fire, emergency, traffic-control,
maintenance, snow-removal, and utility costs to the project rather than to Provo taxpayers,
unless explicitly approved through a transparent public process.

7.     Conservation easements or equivalent legal protections that are recorded, enforceable,
and not dependent on future discretionary promises.

8.    Independent monitoring and enforcement mechanisms, not merely developer
assurances.

The mouth of Provo Canyon is a public-interest location, even where portions of the land are
privately owned. The canyon’s recreational, ecological, transportation, and scenic value extends
far beyond the boundaries of the project site. Because the consequences of this decision could
last for generations, the burden of proof should be high.

I therefore respectfully ask the Planning Commission to recommend denial of the proposed
General Plan amendment and rezoning at this time. I also ask the City Council not to approve
any legislative land-use change until the necessary studies have been completed, reviewed
publicly, and incorporated into enforceable conditions.

A better process would begin with full disclosure of the land ownership and transfer structure,
independent technical studies, clear public-cost accounting, and a narrower land-use proposal
tied to specific, enforceable commitments. Reclamation of the gravel pit may be desirable, but
broad commercial rezoning at the mouth of Provo Canyon is not justified without that level of
analysis.

Thank you for your service to Provo and for considering this comment.

Respectfully,

John Bingham

Provo City resident of 21 years
