Christian Olivetti – June 8

Hello Provo Council & Planning Commission,

I am writing to express my concerns regarding the proposed Vesper Amphitheater project in
Provo Canyon.
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                            *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                              Page 89

This is not a question of whether Utah should have live music, entertainment, or economic
development. The question is whether one of the Wasatch Front's most valuable natural
recreation corridors is the appropriate location for a large-scale entertainment venue and the
development that may follow.

Provo Canyon is one of the few remaining places where residents can quickly escape the noise,
traffic, and pace of urban life. Families come here to picnic, fish, hike, bike, camp, climb, and
spend time together in nature. It is a place where people reconnect with each other, find peace,
and enjoy a landscape that has remained largely undeveloped despite the rapid growth
surrounding it.

Its value comes from what it is today, not from what it could become.

Before approving a project of this magnitude, the community should carefully consider whether
the benefits outweigh the permanent changes that would be required to transform a natural
canyon environment into a major entertainment destination.

Utah already has numerous venues capable of hosting large concerts and events:

•      LaVell Edwards Stadium (Provo) – Capacity: 62,073 – Approx. 25-35 acres

•      Marriott Center (Provo) - Capacity: ~19,000 - Approx. 3 acres

•      Rice-Eccles Stadium (Salt Lake City) – Capacity: 51,444 – Approx. 20-30 acres

•      America First Field (Sandy) – Capacity: 20,213+ – 23 acres

•      Delta Center (Salt Lake City) – Capacity: Approximately 20,000 for concerts – Approx.
10 acres

•     Utah First Credit Union Amphitheatre (West Valley City) – Capacity: 20,000 – Approx. 60
acres

These facilities already exist in locations specifically designed to support large crowds, parking
demand, traffic management, emergency access, lighting, and noise associated with major
events. Provo Canyon serves a fundamentally different purpose.

Unlike urban entertainment districts, the canyon provides a public resource that cannot be
recreated elsewhere. If another concert venue is needed, it can be built in many locations
throughout the valley. Provo Canyon, however, exists in only one place.

For this reason, residents deserve clear and transparent answers to several important
questions:

•      What demonstrated need exists for another venue of this size?

•      How frequently are existing venues operating at or near capacity?

•      Why are current facilities insufficient to meet projected demand?
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                            *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                              Page 90

•      If existing venues can accommodate 20,000 to 60,000 attendees on significantly smaller
and already-developed sites, why is it necessary to transform a natural canyon environment into
another large entertainment destination?

•        How much land will be permanently altered through grading, excavation, roads, parking
lots, utilities, and supporting infrastructure?

•        What wildfire risk assessments have been conducted for the project, and how will large
events, increased vehicle traffic, electrical infrastructure, and human activity affect wildfire
ignition risks in a canyon environment that is already vulnerable to fire? Studies throughout the
western United States have shown that human activity is responsible for the vast majority of
wildfire ignitions. How have these risks been incorporated into emergency response planning,
evacuation procedures, and long-term mitigation strategies?

•     What impacts will increased traffic have on canyon visitors, public safety, emergency
response, and recreational access during peak seasons?

•       How will noise, lighting, and event traffic affect residents, recreation users, and the
natural character of the canyon? Numerous studies have found that chronic noise and artificial
nighttime lighting can disrupt wildlife behavior, migration patterns, feeding, breeding, and habitat
use. Research published by the National Park Service and peer-reviewed ecological studies has
documented that noise pollution can alter bird communication, reduce wildlife occupancy in
otherwise suitable habitat, and increase stress responses in animals. Artificial lighting has
similarly been shown to affect nocturnal species, insects, birds, and other wildlife that depend
on natural light cycles. What analysis has been conducted regarding impacts to the Provo River
ecosystem, local fish populations, and the recreational fishing experience? Will anglers, hikers,
campers, cyclists, and other canyon users still be able to enjoy the quiet natural environment
that makes Provo Canyon unique, or will frequent large-scale events fundamentally alter that
experience?

•      What studies have been conducted regarding habitat fragmentation, wildlife
displacement, water quality, erosion, and long-term environmental impacts?

•      How will a development of this scale address the urban heat island effect created by
large parking lots, roads, buildings, and other paved surfaces? On hot summer days, expansive
paved areas can become significantly hotter than surrounding natural landscapes, increasing
temperatures for visitors, nearby recreation areas, and local wildlife. What mitigation measures,
such as shade trees, landscape buffers, reflective materials, permeable surfaces, or other
cooling strategies, are being considered to offset these impacts?

•       What future development pressures will accompany the venue? Will supporting
infrastructure eventually lead to additional commercial, retail, lodging, or residential
development in areas that are currently undeveloped? Public statements surrounding the
project have led some residents to question whether the venue is intended to serve as an
anchor for broader development that could include luxury accommodations, villas, shopping
areas, or other commercial uses. Residents deserve a clear understanding of the long-term
vision for the area before irreversible changes are made.
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                             *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                               Page 91

•       How will average Provo families benefit from sacrificing a portion of the canyon that is
currently available to everyone for recreation, solitude, and enjoyment of nature?

Many residents are concerned that projects of this scale rarely remain isolated. Large
entertainment venues often become catalysts for additional infrastructure and development.
Roads, parking lots, retail centers, lodging, and residential projects frequently follow. The public
deserves to understand the full long-term vision before any irreversible decisions are made.

This proposal also raises important questions about who benefits from the transformation of
public natural spaces.

Provo Canyon currently belongs to everyone. Families of all income levels can access it for little
or no cost. People come to walk, fish, picnic, ride bicycles, hike trails, enjoy scenic views, and
spend time outdoors. These experiences are available every day and are not dependent on
purchasing tickets or attending events.

How will this project improve access to nature for ordinary residents?

How will it preserve the experiences that make Provo Canyon special today?

How will future generations benefit if portions of the canyon are converted from natural open
space into a large commercial entertainment destination?

The decision before the city is not simply whether to approve a venue. The decision is whether
a unique natural resource should be transformed to accommodate a use that can already be
accommodated elsewhere.

A concert venue can be built in many places.

Provo Canyon exists in only one.

Thank you for your consideration.

Christian Olivetti

Kristina Karalic – June 9

Dear Members of the Planning Commission,

 I am writing regarding the proposed Vesper Amphitheater at the mouth of Provo Canyon (6622
US-189). I have lived in Utah Valley for eight years. When I first moved here, I lived in close
proximity to the canyon and spent days exploring it. Provo Canyon is where I fell in love with this
place. Today I bring my children there to hike and to experience what makes this area special.
My husband regularly uses the gun range near the proposed site. This canyon is part of our
family's daily life, not an abstraction.

I support restoring the gravel pit. That land should be healed. However, what is being requested
goes far beyond gravel pit restoration, and the public deserves to understand the actual scope.

The rezoning application covers approximately 74 acres of city-owned land currently zoned as
Open Space, Preservation and Recreation (OSPR), Public Facilities (PF), and Agricultural
(A1.5). The developers are requesting a rezone to SC3, Regional Shopping Center Zone, as
defined in Provo City Code Chapter 14.20. The project boundary map shows this rezone
extending well beyond the disturbed gravel pit into intact forested canyon hillside. Reclassifying
public forest and open space as a Regional Shopping Center is not restoration.

The city's own Development Services Director, Bill Peperone, stated at the June 2
neighborhood meeting that 95% of the commercial uses permitted under SC3 are uses the city
would not want at this location. A development agreement may limit uses today, but
development agreements can be amended. The zone classification itself is what grants the legal
rights, and it outlasts any agreement.
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                           *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                             Page 94

I have two primary concerns:

First, traffic and emergency access. US-189 already experiences daily mile-long backups
heading west at the 800 North interchange, documented by residents on June 3, 2026, on a
normal Tuesday afternoon. There is one road in and out of Provo Canyon. During a 20,000-
person event, how will emergency vehicles reach canyon residents, hikers, families, and
recreationists? What is the evacuation plan if a wildfire ignites during a sold-out event? These
are not hypothetical risks in a fire-prone canyon corridor. No independent traffic or evacuation
study has been completed.

Second, the transfer of public land. The developers own only 24 to 26 of the approximately 110
acres involved. They are asking Provo City to hand over public forest and open space land for
private commercial use. Has an independent appraisal been conducted? What are taxpayers
giving up, and what are they getting in return?

The developer's own spokesperson acknowledged on June 2 that studies on traffic, emergency
access, parking, sound, lighting, water, wildlife, geology, drainage, and architecture remain
incomplete. I respectfully ask: why would the city rezone public land to SC3 before this work is
done?

Communities across the country have restored quarries and gravel pits into public parks and
conservation land without large-scale commercial development. Three Oaks Recreation Area in
Crystal Lake, Illinois restored a 450-acre gravel quarry into a public recreation area for $14.37
million. Shalersville, Ohio permanently conserved 550 acres of former gravel mine with $2.9
million in state and federal grants. Provo Canyon deserves the same consideration.

I request that the Planning Commission recommend denial of the zone change and General
Plan amendment until independent, publicly reviewed studies are completed on traffic,
emergency access, evacuation, noise, lighting, water quality, wildlife, and taxpayer costs. The
burden of proof belongs on the project, not on residents asking reasonable questions about a
permanent change to one of Utah Valley's most important natural corridors.

Thank you for your service and for receiving my comment.

Respectfully,

Kristina Karalic

Lori Buss – June 9

Council members,

As a long-time resident of Provo Canyon, I am adamantly opposed to the proposed rezoning to
accommodate the Vesper Amphitheater development.

While the project promises commercial and recreational benefits, those potential gains must be
carefully weighed against the very real risks to Provo Canyon’s environment, character, and
quality of life. The canyon’s unique ecology, geology, and identity could be permanently affected
by increased traffic congestion, infrastructure demands, environmental impacts, and light and
noise pollution. The studies that Vesper and Mayor Judkins have committed to conducting may
ultimately confirm these concerns.

Anyone who regularly travels through Provo Canyon already knows that the transportation
infrastructure is stretched to its limits. On many weekends, traffic backs up from 800 North to
Canyon Glen Park, creating long delays on the canyon’s narrow roads. Adding thousands of
additional vehicles for concerts and large events would likely turn an already strained situation
into chronic gridlock. Beyond the inconvenience, increased vehicle traffic would result in higher
levels of air pollution, negatively affecting both residents and visitors while also degrading
habitat and threatening the health of the wildlife that depend on this canyon ecosystem.

I agree that the existing gravel mine is not an ideal long-term use for this area. However,
replacing one incompatible use with another is not the answer. We should be exploring
solutions that preserve and enhance the natural character of Provo Canyon rather than further
commercializing it.
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                           *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                             Page 96

Conserve Utah Valley has urged the Provo City Planning Commission and City Council to

protected through a conservation easement. Even if the final footprint of the amphitheater has
not yet been determined, significant portions of the property could and should remain in OSPR
status.

it is rarely recovered. Any decision on rezoning should prioritize protecting the canyon’s natural
heritage, scenic beauty, and livability for future generations rather than introducing a
development that could fundamentally and permanently alter the character of the canyon.

amphitheater.

Tom Buss – June 10

Council Members,

I am a long-time resident of Provo Canyon, and I respectfully urge you to reject the proposed
rezoning request for the Vesper Amphitheater project.

I understand the desire to improve the current use of the property and create new opportunities
for recreation and economic activity. However, I believe this proposal asks the community to
accept significant and potentially irreversible impacts to one of the most unique and treasured
areas in our region.

Provo Canyon is far more than a potential venue site. It is a scenic gateway, a recreational
destination, a wildlife corridor, and home to residents who value its natural beauty and peaceful
character. The canyon’s appeal comes from its open space, dark skies, and relatively
undeveloped landscape. Rezoning land currently designated for preservation and recreation
purposes would move us in the opposite direction.
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                           *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                             Page 98

One of my greatest concerns is traffic. Anyone who regularly drives through Provo Canyon
knows the existing transportation system is already struggling to handle current demand. During
many weekends and peak recreation periods, traffic routinely backs up from 800 North all the
way to Canyon Glen Park, creating long delays and frustrating congestion. Introducing
thousands of additional vehicles for concerts and special events would place even greater strain
on roads that have limited capacity and few alternatives. This raises concerns not only about
convenience, but also about emergency access, public safety, air quality, and the overall visitor
experience.

I appreciate that studies have been proposed to evaluate transportation, environmental impacts,
and project feasibility. However, those studies should be completed before any zoning changes
are approved, not afterward. Once land is rezoned, the pressure to move a project forward often
increases, regardless of what subsequent studies reveal.

While I agree that the existing gravel mining operation is not necessarily the best long-term use
for this property, replacing it with a large-scale commercial entertainment venue is not the only
option. We should be looking for solutions that better align with the canyon’s natural setting and
preserve its unique character for future generations.

I am also concerned about removing land from Open Space, Preservation, and Recreation
zoning before it is clear how much of the property may ultimately remain undeveloped or be
protected through conservation measures. If portions of the property are worthy of long-term
preservation, they should remain protected rather than being converted to commercial zoning.

Provo Canyon is one of Utah Valley’s most valuable natural assets. Decisions affecting its future
should be made with caution and with an emphasis on conservation, stewardship, and the long-
term interests of residents and visitors alike. Once open space is developed, it is rarely restored
to its original condition.

For these reasons, I respectfully ask that you deny the proposed rezoning request for the Provo
Canyon amphitheater project.

Sincerely,

Tom Buss

Cynthia Scott – June 11

The question is not whether the gravel pit should be restored. The question is whether the
proposed Vesper Amphitheater project will adversely affect the important ecosystems unique to
the bottom of Provo Canyon as a transition point from the canyon to the valley and other
canyons and benches. We might question the validity of it being a “restoration project”.

The presence of an amphitheater will amplify noise, lighting, traffic, dust and runoff. It will
increase the disturbance of wildlife movement, produce light that will confuse nocturnal birds
and animals, and the effects of dust and dirt will create murkiness in the river making it harder
for aquatic plants to flourish and harder for delicate fish eggs to be hatched and thrive.

Canyons act like funnels and pinch points. Ecological impacts are not measured only by how
many acres they are built on. They are measured by how wildlife, water, and natural systems
respond to construction, heat, noise and increased traffic . The proposed Vesper Amphitheater
would introduce thousands of visitors, heavy traffic, lighting, and amplified sound to the entrance
of Provo Canyon, not to mention intensify traffic to an already clogged road.

Ecologists know that habitat loss extends beyond the land directly developed. The boundary
between developed and natural land creates “edge effects”—changes in noise, light,
temperature, human presence, and predator behavior that can degrade habitat adjacent natural
areas. A large venue does not just affect the site itself; it changes the ecological conditions of
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                           *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                            Page 100

the surrounding landscape. Once these ecological functions are lost, they are extremely difficult
to restore.

Many years ago I conducted surveys for the US Fish and Wildlife Service for proposed
construction sites and am familiar with how vulnerable and irreversible construction can affect

Elliott Nelson – June 11

Hello Development Services,

Thank you for reply to my concerns. I have been visiting and studying the site and have a more
focused set of questions/concerns around the size/practicality of parking proposed for the site
and the lack of furnished details.

The main issue is that 8,000 parking spaces take up too large an area for this site - either with
or without a parking garage. Extrapolations from similar sized parking show the parking area
could range from 41 to 75 acres. The developers have not yet provided site plans that show
how parking would fit on the site, with 8,000 planned parking stalls.

Vesper developers are planning a mix of parking – a multi-level parking garage plus surface
parking. A more precise site map of the parking would be helpful to demonstrate how it would
work in practice for a capacity event. Current renderings do not clearly show how 8,000 spaces
would be allocated over the site and how cars would enter and leave the site.

I believe more details are needed to show how parking and traffic will work before the City of
Provo grants any permits.

Here are several problems with the size of this venue and the required parking.

•      8,000 spaces would occupy at least 41 acres of the 100-acre site, using SLC airport as a
model (with a garage). The SLC lots (garage + surface parking) occupy 35 acres with a total of
6,800 spaces - 17.6% smaller than the proposed 8K for Vesper (35 acres x 1.176 = 41 acres).
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                            *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                             Page 101

•       Imagine a parking garage the size of SLC’s garage on the Vesper site. It would occupy a
significant amount of space and be a very visible structure on the site, viewed from the highway
as well as from within Vesper

•      The developers claim that they would bury a large portion of the Vesper garage to
reduce the profile on the site. SLC’s garage cost $165 million to build (all above ground). City
Creek in Salt Lake City has 5,000 underground spaces and cost $178 million to construct in
2012.

•       Entering and leaving a parking structure of this scale will take a long time. It is for this
reason that parking garages are not typically used for events. The largest multi-level parking
structures in the U.S. primarily serve airports and theme parks, rather than traditional sports and
event venues, which typically rely on sprawling, open-air surface lots.

•     The financials don’t work for such an infrequently used parking garage. Airport and
theme park garages are used 365 days a year.

•       If Vesper chooses to go to less expensive all-surface parking, standard surface parking
for 8,000 stalls would require 70-75 acres (assumes ~110-120 spaces per acre, with two-way
driving aisles), or 55-60 acres (assumes smaller spaces and tighter turning radiuses, yielding up
to 140 spaces per acre). Will Vesper go for high or low density?

•       Either way – with a garage or all-surface – the developers need to show how parking of
this scale will fit on the site as it will take more than the above area estimates given the hilly
topography of the site.

On top of the parking questions, there is the issue of accessing the site from only 2
entrances/exits, situated between 2 blind curves on a high-speed highway, the only artery
between Provo and Heber Valley. The City of Provo should also ask the City of Heber and
commuters what they think of the proposed location.

Vesper amphitheater would undoubtedly bring great economic benefit to the city of Provo, but
these important questions need to be addressed before the city settles on this site as being the
best place for the venue. An alternative would be to build a much smaller venue with parking
that better fits the site and doesn’t require a parking structure. If developers insist on a 20K
person amphitheater, it would be best to locate it elsewhere.

Best regards,

Elliott

Provo

restored as low-impact public open space, not commercialized.

Tiffany Blake – June 12

Dear Provo City Council and Provo Planning Commission,

As a local resident, I am writing to express my strong opposition to the proposed Zone Map and
General Plan Amendment for the Vesper Amphitheater project (Items 5-7 on the June 24
Planning Commission agenda).

While I agree that transitioning the current gravel pit away from industrial mining is a positive
goal, the sheer scale of a 20,000-seat venue is entirely incompatible with the ecology, safety,
and identity of Provo Canyon. Funneling that volume of people and vehicles into a narrow
canyon corridor will cause unprecedented gridlock, strain city infrastructure, and create severe
acoustic and light pollution that threatens local wildlife.

I want to state clearly that I fully support Conserve Utah Valley (CUV) in their stance on this
project, and I echo every single question and concern they have officially submitted regarding
location, infrastructure, and environmental impact. The city must demand answers to these
critical inquiries before moving forward.

I am not opposed to smart, canyon-sensitive development, but 20,000 seats is simply too much.
If the goal is to build an entertainment venue that is truly "organic to the canyon," we should look
to Utah’s own highly successful precedent: the Tuacahn Center for the Arts in Ivins.

Tuacahn proves that a venue does not need stadium-sized crowds to be a world-class,
economically viable cultural destination. Consider the following points of comparison:

 Appropriate Scale: While Tuacahn feels grand against its red-rock backdrop, its outdoor
amphitheater actually caps capacity at just 2,500 seats. A sold-out night at Tuacahn is one-
eighth the size of the 20,000-person crowd proposed for Provo Canyon.

 Proven Environmental Safeguards: Because Tuacahn was built in a sensitive habitat, it
required strict, upfront environmental accountability, including wildlife underpasses to protect
migration. Provo Canyon—a crucial corridor for local wildlife—deserves an even more rigorous,
upfront ecological review before any rezone is granted.

 Harmonious Design: Tuacahn’s architecture blends into the canyon rather than dominating it,
utilizing the natural cliffs as a backdrop rather than blocking the landscape with massive
concrete stadium tiers.
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                              *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                               Page 104

A downscaled venue modeled after Tuacahn’s modest footprint would achieve the goals of
restoration, trail connectivity, and economic investment without completely overwhelming the
natural sanctuary that hundreds of thousands of Utah Valley residents enjoy every year.

I urge the Planning Commission and City Council to reject the current Shopping Center (SC3)
rezone request unless the developers commit to a vastly reduced, canyon-appropriate capacity.
Furthermore, I ask that any approvals be strictly conditional, requiring full, public-facing traffic
and wildlife migration studies before any zoning changes take effect.

Thank you for your time, your service, and your dedication to protecting the natural heritage of
Utah Valley

Tiffany Blake

Stephen Done – June 14

Please read the following and decide: is the Vesper Amphitheater proposal really “restoration”
and a “need”, or is it a thneed?

# A Cautionary Canyon Tale

### *(told in the bouncing spirit of a certain mustachioed warning — but written fresh)*

Down where the Provo runs cold through the stone, where the canyon keeps quiet and leaves
you alone, where Timpanogos wears snow like a crown, and the evening slips gentle and easy
on down —

one morning I climbed up the cool canyon air, and the beauty! The beauty was just sitting there:

the aspens, the pines, and the ridgelines of gold, and a hush so enormous it never gets told.

The deer moved like dancers through meadow and fern.

A canyon wren sang where the switchbacks all turn.

The river kept time with a ripple and hum, and I thought, *“Oh, what wonderful country I’ve
come*

*—to adore! To be smitten! I LOVE it!”* I cried.

*“I love every aspen and every hillside!* *And I’ll prove that I love it — yes, that’s what I’ll do!* *I
know the exact perfect thing to pursue.”*

So I unrolled my blueprints. I sent for a crane.

I poured a great parking lot, big as a plain.

With masterful skill and remarkable speed, I bulldozed a stage. (It was something I’d need.)
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                               *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                                Page 109

I hoisted the speakers. I aimed every light straight up at the mountain I loved, through the night.

I printed the tickets. I jammed every lane.

I called it a marvel. I named it — how vain —

**VESPER.** A *gift* to the canyon, I said, while the quiet that lived here for ages lay dead, for a
hush can’t be heard over ten thousand cars, or a bassline that buries the song of the stars.

The deer? They moved off. And the wren? She withdrew.

The stillness packed up, having nothing to do.

*“But I LOVE it!”* I hollered above the loud crush.

*“I treasure this place!”* I announced to the hush.

And THAT is the danger of loving this way:

you can love a thing flat. You can love it away.

You can pave what you praise, you can drown what you prize, and still call it *“love”* with a tear
in your eyes.

So if anyone tells you, all earnest and grand, *“It’s BECAUSE the view’s gorgeous I’m clearing
this land;* *it’s BECAUSE that I love it I’m flooding the night”* — go down to the river while still it
sounds right.

Go stand by the water. Go breathe the cold pine.

Go listen while listening is still something fine.

For a canyon that’s floodlit and ticketed and crammed isn’t *loved.* It’s just *used* — and then
quietly damned.

There’s a kinder way, friend, if your love is sincere:

let the canyon stay canyon. Just… **leave it right here.**



Tanei Henry – June 14

The lack of development in Provo Canyon is a gift to each individual who enters the canyon.
Whether you live in Provo or are visiting for a short time, the absence of billboards,
disturbances, and artificial visuals serves our brains and our hearts. In an ever changing
society, it is a wonder to be surrounded by geology that is millions of years old. Let us each
continue to be students of what Provo Canyon has to offer us just as she is.

My name is Tanei Atagi Henry. In 2016,when my husband and I decided we were going to live
in Provo long term, I decided to get to know the nature around us. That decision has changed
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                           *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                            Page 110

everything for the better. I learned to ski at Sundance. I took my daughters on weekly
adventures to South Fork Park, Big Springs, or Cascade Springs. They developed their own
way of moving among the trees and in and around the rivers. We hiked a bit and stopped to play
as they felt compelled. When their brothers were born, they passed their favorite games and
rituals on to them. Much of our family culture revolves around these beautiful and easy to
access parks. As a young mom then, these parks helped me by providing a place for my kids to
be free and to learn about themselves. They crossed the big logs at South Fork, taking on age-
appropriate challenges. They played in the water at Big Springs, looked at the bugs, and
identified the birds. They learned to care for nature as we packed out our garbage. As they have
grown, we have hiked Stewart Falls, Primrose Overlook, and have a plan with my brother and
his kids to hike Mt. Timpanogos in the future.

We are still in Provo Canyon a lot, building more and more memories. We started camping at
Nunn's Park with our closest friends. We take every visitor into the canyon; Bridal Veil for the
short visitors, Big Springs if we have a few hours, Stewart Falls with a quick stop at the
Sundance Deli for when we have a full day. And we finally, just last summer, learned how to
float Provo River. What a magical time together! I have told my husband for years that "My
retirement plan is to start fly fishing on the Provo River." Everytime I see a fly fisherman out
there, I am jealous of what they are seeing. Provo Canyon, in its current state, provides for the
mental and emotional health of all of us. Whether we recognize it or not. I now live on Canyon
Road with the best access to the canyon I could afford. I love it immensely.

All this to say, an amphitheater can go somewhere else, but this nature cannot. An
amphitheater is not a necessity in Provo Canyon, so I don't even feel the need to provide
solutions for where it might go. I am a regular concert attendee. I am so happy to drive to Delta
Center (yay, Brandi Carlile!) for the biggest shows, where they already have traffic and parking
sorted. Every summer, for years, I have driven to Utah First Amphitheater for a show or two (I
have tickets to Jack Johnson later this year). And the beauty at the Red Butte Garden Summer
series is an absolute treat (I scored tickets to two shows there later this summer).

As I am typing this, I am realizing, it is my pleasure and delight to have my love of concerts and
my love of nature separate. I did not choose to live near a giant amphitheater for many reasons
(traffic, noise, extra garbage). Logistically, Provo is not prepared for the type of amphitheater
Vesper has proposed. Where would the traffic go and parking? What about the extra garbage?
How would local liquor and marijuana laws be enforced with the crowd an amphitheater brings?

It is inevitable that there would be nights where Timpview football has a game, BYU Football
has an event, Vesper Amphitheater has an event and BYU Marriot Center all on the same night.
How are we prepared for that...we are not!

The amphitheater proposed by Vesper would inherently change Provo Canyon. It brings to mind
Joni Mitchell's lyrics from Big Yellow Taxi, "They paved paradise to put up a parking lot." Let's
just don't do it. Vesper can figure out a new project elsewhere.

Jes Scoville – June 16

Dear Provo City Planning Commission:

I live in Provo, Utah, and I love it.

It’s not a town on most people’s list of must-see places, but, let me explain my raging love for
this place.

It has roots in Colorado, where I had a charmed childhood, spending hours after school on my
pink and purple Huffy bike endlessly exploring — watching birds and bugs and streams.
Heading for a certain copse of trees and pretending I was in Terabithia with my loyal Keeshond.
I learned to ride horses, fly over jumps, look forward to the smell of oats mixed with oil. I didn’t
have to pretend to be a mountain girl by wearing a lot of Patagonia: I was a girl and I was in the
mountains. The mountains raised me.

When I left home to attend college in Provo — my first introduction to the place was the
mountains, they jut straight up without any preamble, but their peace had a familiar
homecoming. If my studies got overwhelming, I headed for the hills, just like back home. Usually
Provo Canyon, but sometimes the back road around Utah Lake to watch the pelicans.

Life rolled on, I married, left the west for Cleveland then Ann Arbor, Michigan, followed by San
Antonio and Alexandria, Virginia.

I found little wild refuges in each place, but I missed the mountains with a fierceness that I’m not
sure how to describe. It’s that itch in your soul you just can’t reach, where you know something
is off, but you can’t fix it, can’t satisfy it.
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                             *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                              Page 114

When I had a chance to move back west, I turned in notice and packed up in two weeks. Could
not get here fast enough.

I live close enough to Rock Canyon to head there every morning. The sun slides out from
behind the peak, glancing off the top of nearby Mt. Timpanogos. I brush my fingers along the
rocks, hear the calls of the world waking. The mountains are prayer, they are divinity made
corporeal.

I spent childhood summers up Provo Canyon, college nights and weekends, and now the
canyon is the conduit from the mess of life to the peace of wild things, as Wendell Berry writes.

I want to call Provo Canyon one of my heart’s arteries, but you might think me dramatic. If
anything, it would be understatement.

This is one of my favorite spots near Kamas, Utah. To get here from Provo, your only option is
the road through Provo Canyon.

What do I do when a place I love like part of my body, part of my family, part of my ability to stay
sane on earth, is threatened?

I don’t know! And that’s what’s happening!

I’m devastated by this Vesper Project and how enthusiastic my local officials are about it.

I'm a huge live music fan, but this is the wrong place for a mega-amphitheater, hotel, and
shopping complex. There is only one road in and out of Provo Canyon, and it’s miles from a
freeway. All I can see from this project is loss. Losing birds, plants, insects to pavement. Losing
the sounds of the earth to traffic. Losing access to the Uintahs for the weekend because of the
difficulty getting through the canyon.

I’m not calling myself any kind of expert on gravel pits or city planning or amphitheaters, but I
am calling myself a person who loves the canyon. This project (which is much more moveable
and scalable than a canyon) is not the right thing for the single road from Provo to the wild
spaces to the east. Any time I think about it, I get sick to my stomach. I get depressed for me
and my kids and all the kids and people in this area who won’t be able to get to the mountains
when they need to.

I just finished Hank Lentfer’s memoir Faith of Cranes this week. I think he wrote it for me. He
chronicles his devastation at the devastation of the natural places around him and across the
world to his arrival at a kind of equilibrium, realizing that the most important thing he can do is
love the places that mean something to him.

Here are my attempts to grope for hope in times like these: where the people we’ve elected to
protect not just the city, but the natural world that interweaves and surrounds it, put profit over
any other consideration. (A phenomenon that certainly isn’t limited to my beloved canyon.)
Many thanks to Hank Lentfer for this inspiration:

•      Is the world going to be eclipsed by the gears of the Industrial Revolution that remain
hell-bent on processing beauty into a money-making venture?
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                                 *Item 3
June 24, 2026                                                                                  Page 115

•      What can I do when the wild places around me are threatened by commercial
development without regard to environmental impact?

•       A lot gets buried underneath the pavement.

•       How do we notice and love what’s already there before deciding it should be a hotel?

•      “I [Hank Lentfer] have been motivated, like so many people, by what Scott Russell
Sanders calls the ‘bite of conscience.’ But what happens when the cries swell beyond our ability
to respond? How do we keep from being buried by a swelling sense of failure?”

•        Or what happens when the people we elect to steward the earth can no longer hear the
bite of conscience?

•       Insanity often passes for progress.

•       How do you live a life so that joy remains when all else fades?

•     “Hope, however feeble its foundation, bespeaks allegiance to every unlikely beauty that
remains on earth.” — David James Duncan

•         “The only spiritually responsible way I know to be a citizen, artist, or activist, is by giving
little or no thought to things such as saving the planet, achieving world peace, or stopping
neocon greed. Great things seem undoable things. Small things, lovingly done, are always
within our reach.” — David James Duncan

So, dear Mayor Judkins, the Provo City Planning Commission, the Provo City Council, Vesper
Project Planners, here’s my question: Are you pushing for the Vesper Amphitheater out of love?
Love for this place, for Provo Canyon, for the mountains and the countless living things that call
that place home?

•       If you feel like this is a project born of love, can you show me what you love?

•      I have a feeling that the things you love most about this project are money, money,
development, and cash.

•      Why do you want your legacy in office to be the development of a natural Provo
treasure?

I cannot express how utterly disappointed, heartbroken, and disgusted I will be if this project
goes through.

But, I also pledge to continue in the spirit of Hank Lentfer, no matter the outcome on this project
and countless projects that will likely threaten this same space in the future, to do small acts
with great love.

Who knows where those small seeds of love will plant themselves?

The story of the earth can still be a story of beauty and love, as long as the individuals who still
respect wild places continue to show their love for that wild beauty.

PROPERTY HISTORY
Of the five parcels listed in this concept plan, four are currently owned by the Provo Canyon
Mining Company (the western 34.23 acres); and one is owned by Provo City (the eastern 66.47
acres). The applicant and developer for this proposal has initiated contracts with both parties to
secure the total 100.7 acres of land for an entertainment and hospitality development.

The privately-owned land has had an active mining permit for decades and would close the
operation of that use if this proposal was approved and moved forward. The city land has
mostly remained as open space with natural vegetation and trails, though the adjacent mining
operation to the west and shooting park to the east have impacted some of this land as well.

NEIGHBORHOOD FEEDBACK
The North Timpview Neighborhood held a neighborhood meeting with the developer to provide
information about the proposal on June 1st, 2026. A report of that meeting and feedback
presented is attached to this staff report. Additionally, staff have received several emails prior
to this report that provide opinions and feedback on the proposal. Copies of these public
comments received prior to the report are attached to the rezone staff report (Item #3, June
24th, 2026, Planning Commission, PLRZ20260221).

PROJECT SUMMARY
The proposed project is best described in the applicant’s written statement, attached to this
report. In summary, the developer proposes to create a hub for entertainment with a new
amphitheater, hotel, and variety of retail shops. The applicant, Mr. Bayles, proposes using the
Regional Shopping Center (SC3) zone to accomplish this vision. However, since the SC3 zone
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                          Item 4
June 24, 2026                                                                            Page 2

allows for a wider range of uses than the concept imagines, staff have asked Mr. Bayles to enter
into a development agreement that would limit the permitted uses to only those that are
provided in the concept plan materials and specifically allowing an amphitheater (SLU 7211).
The development agreement also identifies the increased height for a “flyloft”, which would allow
that non-habitable structure to go beyond the standard height limitation of the SC3 Zone, but
only up to 125 feet. This development agreement is attached to the rezone staff report (Item #3,
June 24, 2026, Planning Commission, PLRZ20260221).

                ATTACHMENT 4 – APPLICANT STATEMENT


Vesper Amphitheater is envisioned as a transformational, four-season destination
entertainment venue capable of operating as a fully enclosed winter performance hall
with up to 8,000 seats while converting into a 25,000 max-seat open-air summer
amphitheater.

While the primary use will be the entertainment venue, there will also be up to a hotel
(up to 75-keys), parking garage (up to 10,000 stalls, indoor and outdoor parking),
restaurant, and retail space.

Vesper Amphitheater will establish a new benchmark in adaptable entertainment
architecture in the Mountain West. Located at the gateway to Provo Canyon, the venue
must combine architectural distinction while also creating a unique place for families to
gather and enjoy music and nature together.

       ATTACHMENT 5 – NEIGHBORHOOD MEETING REPORT AND
                           FEEDBACK

  Resident Concerns re: proposed Vesper Amphitheater
  North Timpview Neighborhood Meeting - June 1, 2026

Overall Project Concerns

   •   Many residents opposed the project or expressed serious reservations about its
       size, location, and long-term impacts.

   •   Several speakers said the proposal appears too large and too fast-moving for such a
       sensitive canyon location.

   •   Residents repeatedly asked the City to slow down, complete studies ﬁrst, and allow
       more public review before any zoning change.

   •   Some questioned whether a 20,000-seat venue is appropriate at the mouth of Provo
       Canyon.

   •   Several comments challenged the framing that the only choices are leaving the
       gravel pit as-is or approving the Vesper proposal.

Traffic, Parking, and Public Safety

   •   Traffic was the most repeated concern.

   •   Residents questioned how 20,000 attendees could safely enter and exit through a
       constrained canyon corridor.

   •   Several people compared the situation to BYU game traffic, USANA Amphitheater
       traffic, and existing congestion during peak canyon use.

   •   Concerns included:

          o   One main road in and out of the canyon

          o   Congestion on Highway 189 and Canyon Road

          o   Neighborhood cut-through traffic

          o   Safety for pedestrians, bikers, runners, pets, and children

          o   Existing crashes and accident risks

          o   Emergency evacuation during wildﬁre or other disasters
Planning Commission Staff Report                                                        Item 4
June 24, 2026                                                                         Page 11

   •   Many asked for detailed traffic studies, UDOT projections, ingress/egress plans, and
       mitigation strategies before approval.

   •   Parking concerns included whether on-site parking is realistic, whether shuttles
       would be used, how many vehicles would be accommodated, and whether parking
       for workers and guests would be sufficient.

Project Scale and Venue Size

   •   Many residents said the proposed venue is too large for the canyon.

   •   Several suggested a smaller venue, such as 5,000 seats or something closer in scale
       to Sundance, Tuacahn, Deer Valley, or Red Rocks.

   •   Some questioned why a 100-acre footprint is needed for a venue comparable in
       seating to the Marriott Center.

   •   Residents raised concerns about the amount of land, paving, parking, and
       infrastructure required for a 20,000-person venue.

   •   Some comments questioned whether the business model requires increasingly
       larger or more frequent events over time.

Zoning, Development Agreement, and Process

   •   Many residents asked that a formal development agreement be completed before
       any zone change is considered. Suggested development agreement components
       included:

          o   Clear allowed uses

          o   Deed restrictions

          o   Required studies

          o   Timelines and benchmarks

          o   Limits on event size and frequency

          o   Enforcement mechanisms

          o   Reversion to current zoning if the project becomes infeasible

   •   Several residents worried that once rezoning occurs, it may be difficult for the City to
       enforce commitments.

   •   Some comments said the process feels backwards and that impact studies should
       come before rezoning.

June 24, 2026                                                                          Page 12

Environmental and Natural Resource Impacts

   •   Residents emphasized that Provo Canyon is a major natural and recreational asset
       for Utah Valley.

   •   Concerns included:

          o   Loss of open space

          o   Deforestation and heat from pavement

          o   Impacts to wildlife habitat

          o   Light pollution and dark-sky impacts

          o   Noise impacts

          o   Water use

          o   Groundwater, aquifers, springs, and ﬂood risk

          o   Construction impacts on nearby natural spaces

   •   Several residents said the canyon should be preserved as open space, not
       converted to commercial use.

   •   Some objected to describing the proposal as “restoration,” arguing that commercial
       development is not the same as restoring the gravel pit.

Alternatives to the Proposal

   •   Many residents asked the City to consider other options besides the Vesper project:

          o   Reclaiming the gravel pit as a natural recreation area

          o   Restoring wildlife habitat or watershed function

          o   Creating a public park

          o   Adding mountain bike trails

          o   Issuing a call for proposals for lower-impact reuse ideas

          o   Purchasing and restoring the gravel pit acreage

   •   Several residents argued that gravel pit reclamation does not require a large
       commercial development.

Open Space, Public Land, and Precedent

   •   Residents expressed support for the current open space preservation and

Noise, Lighting, and Neighborhood Impacts

Recreation and Trail Impacts

Supportive or Mixed Feedback

Dear Members of the Planning Commission,


I want to express my sincere appreciation for the time, diligence, and thoughtful
consideration you have devoted to reviewing the proposed Vesper Amphitheater project.
This is a significant decision for our community, and I am grateful for the care you are
taking to gather information, listen to public input, and thoroughly evaluate the proposal.

This project has received substantial scrutiny from both the public and city officials. I
have received many emails raising important questions about the proposed zone
change, traffic, environmental impacts, infrastructure, open space, sound, lighting,
safety, and the enforceability of commitments made by the developers. These are valid
concerns, and I share the desire to ensure they are fully addressed.

Because of those concerns, I have worked closely with Provo’s legal department, Public
Works Department, and Development Services Department to create a comprehensive
development agreement and real estate contract that, if approved, would legally bind
developers to strict project standards. Together, these documents establish clear
requirements and obligations that must be satisfied before the project can move
forward. If developers fail to deliver on these obligations, any rezone recommended by
the Planning Commission and approved by the City Council would revert to the existing
zoning.

As you continue your review, I would like to highlight two issues that have generated
significant public interest: traffic mitigation and the future of the existing gravel pit.

First, traffic. Provo City’s Public Works Director and Traffic Engineer have been working
with UDOT and the Vesper development team to review traffic studies and identify
mitigation measures. Completion of those studies is required, and all applicable federal,
state, and local standards for safety and traffic operations must be met before UDOT
will even consider allowing project organizers to utilize Highway 189.

Second, the future of the gravel pit. When I first learned about this proposal, I spent time
studying the long-term realities of gravel pit operations along the Wasatch Front. What I
found is that gravel pits rarely disappear on their own. Because aggregate materials
remain in high demand and transportation costs are significant, sites near growing
communities often continue operating for decades through mining, material processing,
and recycling activities. I remember when the Point of the Mountain was a true point,

                                                                                 MAYOR.PROVO.GOV
                                                                                MAYOR’S OFFICE
                                                                                TEL (801) 852-6105
                                                                                445 W CENTER ST
                                                                                PROVO, UT 84601


and a world class hang-gliding destination. Now, despite significant opposition from
Draper City and its residents, the most recognizable landmark between Salt Lake City
and Provo has become a massive gravel mining operation. I hope to avoid the entrance
to our beautiful Provo Canyon suffering a similar fate.

The Provo Canyon gravel pit has been mined for decades and is already functioning as
a material reclamation and recycling site. Gravel mining is protected under state law,
and cities cannot regulate it. The unfortunate reality is that this gravel pit has the
potential for ongoing, increasingly invasive operations for decades to come. Purchasing
an existing gravel pit for restoration is also prohibitively expensive, and Provo City has
yet to see any proposal from a group willing to purchase the land and convert it to
greenspace alone. For that reason, I believe it is important to evaluate this proposal in
the context of the site's realistic alternatives.

The responsibility before you is not simply to consider the appropriateness of a zone
change. It is to determine if our city is willing to entertain a proposal for this land other
than gravel excavation. If a zone change is passed, Provo City is not approving the
amphitheater project. It is, however, signaling a willingness to carefully consider and
review a future proposal that may transform an existing industrial site into a destination
that provides recreation, entertainment, tourism, economic activity, and protected open
space for the community.
Reasonable people may disagree about the merits of the Vesper project, and it is
entirely appropriate to apply strict scrutiny and high expectations to project plans.
However, opportunities to convert an active gravel pit into a significant community asset
are exceedingly rare. I believe it is important that this opportunity be evaluated with a
full understanding of both the project's challenges and its potential benefits.

Thank you again for your service, professionalism, and commitment to making
decisions that will shape Provo's future for generations to come.



Sincerely,




Mayor Marsha Judkins

Andrew Maxﬁeld | June 23, 2026
Yes to arts & entertainment. NO to Vespers, please.

Dear Provo City Council & Planning Commission,

I'm writing as a Provo citizen who loves performing arts/entertainment, loves great venues,
and who is against the proposed Vespers Amphitheater.

I'm sure you've heard plenty of concerns about parking, traffic, noise, possible detriments
to Provo Canyon and our shared ecosystem, etc.

I'd like to offer another angle: the venue's business model is probably impossible.

Venues are tricky, regardless of scale, regardless of whether they are privately held (like
USANA, The Depot, Delta Center, etc) or publicly owned (Covey, SCERA, Eccles, Abravanel,
etc). A venue of this scale and expense could probably only be viable in the hands of an
entity like Live Nation, and probably only if 200+ dates per year were sold at champagne
stadium prices. Think about that. And then remember that I can ﬂy to Vegas from Provo
next weekend to see Kenny Chesney at The Sphere.

Something of this magnitude could be a black eye if it succeeds and an even bigger black
eye if it fails: who holds the bag when a facility of this scale and scope can't sustain itself in
the long run?

If, as they say in medicine, "dosage determines the poison," then I'd argue that the scale of
this proposal, and its implied business model, is probably poison.

Meanwhile, if Donny Osmond—or any of the investor-developers—would like to make a
positive mark (and proﬁt), there are probably opportunities of an appropriate scale and
location, which could ﬁll an actual void in the marketplace of arts/entertainment
experiences and leave a legacy that isn't a black eye.

Thanks,

Andrew Maxﬁeld



Cynthia Stott | June 23, 2026
Zoning Change

Even with strong development agreements and later conditions, a rezoning decision still
changes what the land is legally allowed to become. Once land currently designated as
open space, public land, or agricultural land is rezoned to commercial use, the range of
future outcomes is fundamentally expanded.
While mitigation measures, conservation easements, and phased approvals are important
tools, they do not fully restore or preserve existing ecological function or prevent long-term
intensiﬁcation of use within the newly allowed zoning scope.

For that reason, I would respectfully ask that the Council consider not only whether
conditions can manage impacts, but whether the initial shift in zoning is appropriate for
land that is currently functioning as part of the canyon–valley transition zone.

Thanks for considering this comment.

Cynthia Stott

Provo resident



Joanna Harmon | June 23, 2026
Vesper Project Comment

I am a long-time resident of Provo with many concerns about the proposed Vesper project
in Provo Canyon.

1. Traffic

How is a proposed 8,000-20,000 person venue going to affect traffic on university Ave and
800 N in Orem? I work on 800 N in Orem, and traffic is already bad at the end of the day for
those heading north on University ave.

Would this project include widening roads?

2. Pollution: Noise, air, and light.

Many people hike and camp along trails through Rock Canyon and Provo Canyon. Imagine
trying to enjoy a quiet night of camping with a large music venue just a few miles away. Not
only would you hear the venue, but the lights would obscure stars, and the emissions from
tens of thousands of extra cars traveling to and from would affect air quality.

3. Wildlife and environmental impacts

How will this project affect local ecosystems?

I know the current site is a former extraction pit, and improving the site is desirable.
However, the proposed venue is NOT a restoration project, as some have tried to frame it. It
is a construction project with major implications for residents and wildlife. Restoration
would look more like native habitat—plants and trees that would invite local insects, birds,
and other wildlife. Don’t let developers use the word restoration unless they are putting
nature back to what it was before.

4. Proposed size

The proposed 8,000-20,000 seat amphitheater is too big a range to consider seriously. Red
Rocks in Colorado seats around 9500, while the BYU Marriott Center seats around 19,000.
The difference in scope represents very different impacts on all the areas of concern
mentioned above. In my opinion, the proposal is too vague and unrealistic to be the basis
of a zoning change.

Before the council and commissioners consider land use changes to facilitate this venue,
more research needs to be done. Yes, this is privately owned property, but for a project this
huge that will affect residents for decades to come, please take your time in researching
the impacts.

I appreciate your service to our community and your sincere efforts to improve quality of
life for all. Please consider seriously my concerns and those of others that you hear.

Thank you,

Joanna Harmon

Annie Bryner | June 23, 2026
Import questions the public wants answered before you recommend rezoning

Hello,

My name is Dr. Annie Bryner. I have spent a great deal of time compiling the important
questions that need to be answered and even some concerns about red ﬂags that I see as a
therapist.

I respectfully ask that you please take the time to read through and consider these

Sincerely,
Dr. Annie Bryner, PhD, LCSW, CST

To: the planning commission and the Provo City Council
From: Annie Bryner, PhD, LCSW, CST
Re: Vesper Amphitheater Rezoning: 101 Questions That Should Be Answered Before Any
Zoning Change Is Approved

I am not opposed to concerts, economic development, tourism, or the Osmond family.

I am opposed to making a major zoning change before the public has sufficient information
to understand the consequences of that decision.

According to Provo City's own published information, the proposed Vesper Amphitheater
project is not approved, requires multiple future approvals, requires substantial studies
that have not yet been completed, and does not yet have a ﬁnalized development
agreement. The city has stated that traffic mitigation, infrastructure needs, environmental
impacts, sound impacts, and lighting impacts remain under review.

The question before the Planning Commission is not whether concerts are enjoyable. The
issue is not whether Utah needs another concert venue. The issue is whether the entrance
to one of the most iconic canyons in Utah should be transformed from a gateway to
recreation into a gateway to commercial entertainment. The question is whether the public
has enough information to justify converting more than 100 acres of land from agricultural,
open-space, recreation, and public-facility designations into commercial zoning.

This cannot be one of the situations where the city council designates land to be disposed
of and then it is sold to developers for pennies on the dollar, as has happened in too many
council meetings over the last decade. The Provo residents are fatigued. We are tired of the
constant construction and the building of apartments on almost every empty space in our
city without our quality of life being considered. That is what makes the Vespar project even
more egregious.

People are also concerned that you're considering doing business with people who tried to
stick the tax payers with a billion-dollar bill to relocate the State Hospital so they could
expand Splash Summit. The people deserve to expect the city to protect them from people
who have shown a lack of principles on other land deals.

Please don’t forget; the eyes of the entire State of Utah are upon you! The Planning
Commission is not merely evaluating a single amphitheater. It is establishing a precedent
regarding the future use of Utah's canyon corridors.

For generations, Utah's canyons have served as public recreational resources. Families use
them for hiking, cycling, ﬁshing, camping, skiing, climbing, wildlife viewing, photography,
and quiet enjoyment of the outdoors.

The question before the Commission is whether major commercial entertainment districts
should become an accepted use at the entrances to these canyon systems.

If a 20,000-25,000-seat amphitheater, hotel, restaurants, retail development, and
associated infrastructure are appropriate at the mouth of Provo Canyon, what principle
would prevent similar proposals at the entrances of other canyons throughout Utah? All
future developers can, and likely will use the decisions that are made regarding the Vespar
project as justiﬁcation to commercialize every canyon.

Land-use decisions are rarely evaluated in isolation. They communicate community values
and establish expectations for future development. The eyes of many developers, who
would like to commercialize Utah canyons are also on you; really on all of us. So many of us
hope that we are not the generation of adults that allow this to happen.

Every major change begins with a statement that it is unique, limited, and unlikely to be
repeated. Yet future applicants inevitably point to prior approvals as evidence that similar
development should also be allowed.

This Commission should consider whether Utah's canyon corridors are primarily public
recreational resources to be preserved for future generations, or commercial opportunities
to be developed when a sufficiently attractive proposal is presented, because you aren’t
just making a decision for Provo Canyon.

Once a canyon entrance is commercialized, that decision is extraordinarily difficult to
reverse. The question is not whether this project is attractive. The question is whether this
is the precedent we wish to establish.

Each generation inherits Utah's canyons from the generation before it. Our responsibility is
not simply to enjoy them, but to determine what we leave for those who come after us. No
canyon becomes commercialized all at once. It happens one approval at a time. The
canyons belong to all Utahns. The proﬁts from this project will belong to a relatively small
number of private parties. Before converting public recreational character into private
commercial opportunity, the burden should be on the applicant to demonstrate
overwhelming public beneﬁt and minimal public cost.

The Burden of Proof Belongs to the Applicant

The applicant should be required to demonstrate that the project can succeed without
creating unacceptable impacts on:

   •   Traffic

   •   Public safety

   •   Water resources

   •   Wastewater systems

   •   Environmental resources

   •   Taxpayers

   •   Existing canyon users

   •   Adjacent property owners

   •   The public’s use of Provo Canyon to not be limited in any way during the years of
       construction

The absence of information is not evidence that these concerns have been solved. We
should not grant a zoning change when there are so many unanswered questions about
their project. There especially should not be zoning change granted for the land owned by
the city, which Vespar clearly intends to acquire. That city land should be used for the
beneﬁt of the people.

The Osmonds Representation of this Huge Project

A simple Google search of “Vespar Ampitheater” shows smiling Osmonds announcing that
the amphitheater WILL be built in Provo Canyon. This is deceptive language and the public
deserves better. In psychology we consider this kind of language as a form of gaslighting,
intending to manipulate opinions and beliefs.

One of their greatest deceptions is the statement that “90% of the residents of Provo are for
this project.” Again, this is a form of gaslighting which appears to try to convince the public
that the project is a “done deal” and that the public should not bother pushing back. A
public who feels “gaslit” is a public who does not trust that this is being done for their
beneﬁt or trust he administration who would approve it. The city should push back against
these tactics, to keep us, the residents safe. And an applicant who uses these tactics does
not appear to have trust in their own project. The public deserves to know how they came
to that 90% number:

   •   What independent survey was conducted?

   •   What was the sample size, methodology, margin of error, geographic distribution of
       respondents?

   •   Who paid for the survey?"

Vespar has treated the public with contempt, refusing to answer questions and then
complaining to city officials that the public decided to ﬁnd their own answers, and publish
their ﬁndings on social media. Even KSL and Deseret News point out the problem of Vespar
refusing to answer questions. It should be no surprise that the people here are not willing to
go along with a mammoth project like this and settle for their questions to be answered
later.

The Osmonds also state that this is a project which is meant to bring families together.
Again, this is a psychological attempt at swaying the public by using language that matters
the most to the majority of Provo residents. We care the most about our families. Today,
families can enjoy Provo Canyon for free. Vespar will come with ticket prices, which, if
comparable with other entertainment venues, will likely be out of the range of many

items in the proposed shops or the food in the restaurants? Exactly how will this bring
families together? If there are free areas for families to walk around in, what is the
likelihood that families will want to compete with 10,000 other cars to get there, and where
will these families park?

Questions Regarding Water

This is probably the #1 concern which should raise a huge red ﬂag with the planning
commission and city council. Utah is in a “mega-drought” which some experts speculate
could last until 2050. We are restricted in how much we can water our lawns. So you may
understand how there is a feeling that Vespar is greedy and insensitive in that they believe
they are entitled to thousands of gallons of the Provo residents’ water every day while we
have to limit our showers and live with dried out lawns. This demonstrates a sense of
entitlement (another huge psychological concern) and will lead to great feelings of
resentment on the part of the residents. We the residents feel we deserve TRUTHFUL
answers to the following questions:

   1. What is the honest projected water demand for:

          o   Use of water amphitheater events?

          o   Water use and waste plans for 20,000 people using restroom facilities?

          o   Water usage for the hotel – typical answers are 200-400 gallons per day, per
              room

          o   Water usage for Restaurants?

          o   Water usage for the retail businesses?

          o   Water usage for the landscaping and irrigation?

   2. What water rights have been secured?

   3. What source will provide the water?

   4. What drought contingency plans exist?

   5. Has an independent water-supply analysis been completed?

   6. Will the analysis be released to the public before rezoning?

   7. How could this possibly not effect the residents’ rights to water? There is only so

Questions Regarding Wastewater

   8. How much wastewater will be generated during a sold-out event?

   9. What treatment facilities will process that much wastewater?

   10. Does existing infrastructure have adequate capacity?

   11. What upgrades will be required?

   12. Who will pay for those upgrades?

Questions Regarding Traffic

   13. How many vehicle trips are expected during a sold-out event?

   14. How many parking spaces are planned?

   15. Where is overﬂow parking located?

   16. What is the estimated queue length before and after events?

   17. What is the estimated travel delay for canyon users?

   21. Will the public be allowed to review all traffic analyses before rezoning?

   22. How much is the traffic impacts to people who are not attending a function at the
       venue being considered?

In just doing some basic math and research, the mouth of Provo Canyon is where Orem’s
800 North and Provo’s University Avenue meet and become US-189; UDOT describes that
junction as the mouth of the canyon. A simple queue estimate:

   •   10,000 cars × about 25 feet per car with spacing = 250,000 feet

   •   250,000 feet ÷ 5,280 = about 47 miles of cars in one lane

   •   Even if split across 2 lanes, that is still about 23 miles of backed up traffic

   •   Split across 4 lanes, still about 12 miles

So, if 10,000 vehicles arrive for a sold-out event, that represents roughly 47 lane-miles of
vehicles before accounting for traffic signals, turning movements, parking checks,
pedestrians, shuttle operations, crashes, weather, or ordinary canyon traffic. Even if those
cars are divided across multiple approach lanes, the queue could easily extend for miles
along University Avenue, 800 North, and potentially toward I-15 unless the applicant can
provide a professional traffic study proving otherwise.

UDOT Federal Highway Administration guidance also notes that intersection capacity
depends on lanes, signal timing, pedestrians/bicycles, upstream/downstream lanes, and
safety constraints—not just optimistic claims about “rapid parking” which we have
received from the applicant.

   23. What proof does Vespar have that their supposed “rapid loading parking system”
       will work and prevent traffic back-ups?

The canyons belong to all Utahns. The proﬁts from this project will belong to a relatively

Most of us that object to the Provo Canyon location for this venue feel that the applicant
has not demonstrated why this particular location is superior to alternative locations with
fewer impacts. Many of the project's perceived beneﬁts appear to derive from the scenery
and dramatic setting of Provo Canyon. However, the Planning Commission's responsibility
is not to determine which location creates the most impressive promotional materials for

proposed use and if the needs and desires of the public are prioritized.

The "We Need Rezoning First" Argument

Some city representatives have suggested that certain studies occur later in the process.
That may be true. However, that does not answer the central question: how can the
Planning Commission determine that commercial zoning is appropriate when the
information necessary to evaluate the consequences of that zoning is not yet available?

We don’t owe them a rezoning or anything else so that they have time to ﬁgure out to dazzle
the decision makers or soften the potential blow of them paying for all the aforementioned
studies that need to happen. Vespar have not been forthcoming to anyone who has
emailed them or phoned them.

Rezoning is not a procedural formality. Rezoning is a policy decision.

If the studies do not yet exist, then the applicant has not yet demonstrated that the site can
safely support the proposed use, or that the public’s desire to use the canyon for their own
personal entertainment is priority.

The prudent response is not approval. The prudent response is to wait until the information
exists.

The public has repeatedly been shown beautiful conceptual renderings. Supporters are

   •      Traffic appears minimal.

   •      The surrounding development appears compact and orderly.

   •      The canyon appears largely unaffected.

   •      There are no visual indications of 20,000–25,000 people arriving or leaving.

   •      Service vehicles

   •   Vendor and truck staging areas
